Emergent causal order and time direction: bridging causal models and tensor networks
Carla Ferradini 1, Giulia Mazzola 1, V. Vilasini 1,2

Abstract
Can the direction of time and the causal structure of space-time be inferred from operational principles? Causal models and tensor networks offer complementary perspectives: the former encodes cause-effect relations via directed graphs, with intrinsic ordering; the latter describes multipartite systems on undirected graphs, without presupposing directionality. We construct two-way mappings between these two frameworks, linking direction agnostic correlation functions and operational notions of signalling. This clarifies the operational meaning of causal influence in tensor networks and introduces discrete “space-time rotations” of causal models which preserve signalling relations. Applying our framework to holographic tensor networks, we use tools from causal inference, like graph-separation, to analyse emergent causal structures. By permitting cyclic and indefinite causal structures, our results enable transfer of techniques across tensor networks and a range of causality frameworks.



1
Introduction

A central question in the foundations of physics is whether the direction of time — and, more generally, properties of space-time — can be inferred rather than assumed a priori. In standard causal reasoning, temporal directionality is built into the formalism: causes precede effects, interventions propagate forward, and explanatory structure reflects this asymmetry. Causal models
[
Pea09
,
BLO20
,
HLP14
,
BLO21
,
FGV25b
,
FM18
,
VR24a
,
VR24b
,
CS16
]
(both classical and quantum) exemplify this viewpoint. They describe relations between processes using directed graphs whose arrows encode causal ordering, and whose building blocks possess a predefined input-output structure. Even in extensions allowing cycles
[
BFPM21
,
BLO21
,
FGV25b
]
, a local orientation remains embedded in the components themselves, so that directionality is part of the description from the outset. Although causality in such models can be formalised from an information-theoretic perspective, without assuming a background space-time, this is done through the connectivity of channels and systems, whose distinction between inputs and outputs induces directionality.

In many areas of physics, however, causal structure plays a different role. In relativity and quantum field theory, causal relations are induced by the geometry of space-time, with light cones determining which events may influence one another. In candidate theories of quantum gravity, space-time itself is expected to be dynamical and possibly subject to quantum superposition, motivating the question of whether causal and geometric structure might emerge from more primitive quantum correlations. Tensor networks
[
CJQW18
,
Vid03
,
VC04
,
LN07
,
QY18
]
, since no causal structure is assumed as a primitive, provide a natural framework in which to explore this possibility. As graphical representations of many-body quantum states and processes, tensor networks are defined on undirected graphs and treat all tensor legs symmetrically, without presupposing any global or local time direction. They encode correlations and compositional structure while remaining agnostic about causal order. In this sense, tensor networks offer a setting in which space-time or causal structure, if present, must emerge rather than be imposed.

Each framework, however, faces certain challenges in addressing the operational emergence of time. In tensor networks, a notion of quantum causal influence based on correlation functions has been proposed
[
CHQY19
]
. While this is an intriguing proposal, its relation to established operational notions of causality and causal reasoning remains unclear. In particular, is not evident whether such definitions capture genuine causal structure or rather reflect patterns of correlation, nor whether they support the kind of operational reasoning characteristic of causal models. Causal models, by contrast, encode directionality at the structural level. While this feature underlies their explanatory power, it is sometimes regarded as conceptually delicate when applied to fundamental physics, where many underlying laws are time-reversal symmetric. From this perspective, the asymmetry of directed graphs may appear to reflect modelling choices — such as the role of interventions or agents — rather than fundamental physical structure. How to understand or adapt such directionality in scenarios with dynamical or indefinite causal order remains an open question.

Addressing these challenges calls for a framework that relates directionally agnostic descriptions of correlations to operational notions of causality. In this work, we establish a precise connection between tensor networks and quantum causal models by constructing explicit mappings between the two. These mappings translate correlation functions in tensor networks into probabilistic quantities in, possibly cyclic, causal models and relate causal influence to signalling relations. By linking a structure without predefined orientation to one in which causal direction is explicit, we provide a setting in which questions about the emergence, interpretation, and operational meaning of time direction can be formulated sharply and analysed systematically, while being able to apply tools from both frameworks consistently. In doing so, we aim to build a bridge between approaches to causal inference and questions on emergent space-time, and to clarify how operational notions of causality can arise within directionally agnostic quantum frameworks.

Figure 1
:
Visual intuition behind main results
Our work defines operationally motivated mappings between (possibly cyclic) quantum causal models and tensor networks, going in both directions. Through this, a causal model on the directed graph
\vec{G}
can be uniquely mapped to a tensor network on the undirected graph
\bar{G}
. From the tensor network on
\bar{G}
, we can obtain four different causal models, one for each choice of directions of edges. The choice associated with the directions of
\vec{G}
will lead again to the original causal model. While, other choices, which lead to causal models on the directed graphs on the right, might require self-loops to give a valid model. Such possibility can be easily checked through evaluating a partial trace. We show that the mappings preserve certain useful properties, allow to interpret alternative causal models obtained through the mapping as “rotations” of the original model, and to apply graph-separation theorems from causal models to analyse tensor networks.

1.1
Summary of contributions and structure of the paper

In this work, we present mappings from causal models to tensor networks and vice versa which link signalling relations in the former to causal influence in the latter. In
Section

2
, we provide a review on tensor network and the definition of causal influence proposed in
[
CHQY19
]
, collecting clarifications and open questions regarding it. In
Section

3
, we review a cyclic causal modelling framework presented in
[
FGV25b
,
FGV25a
]
, together with definitions of interventions and signalling. The main contributions of the paper are the following:

•

Mappings between tensor networks and causal models:
In
Section

4
, we propose concrete mappings which tightly link properties of the two frameworks (see
figure

1
for an illustrated example). The features of these mappings naturally portray the absence of a predefined direction of inputs and outputs in tensor networks: a given causal model can always be uniquely mapped to a tensor network on the same graph deprived of directions (
Section

4.1
); while for tensor networks to causal models we present two mappings, the second being a strict generalisation of the first. The first mapping (
Section

4.2
) preserves the graph structure of the tensor network up to augmenting it with direction, but, depending on the tensors of the network can lead to one, multiple or no causal models. The generalised mapping (
Section

4.2.2
), on the other hand, allows to construct a valid causal model for any choice of directions up to introducing cyclicity.
Finally, in
Section

4.3
we investigate properties of these mappings.

•

Operational Causal Influence:
In
Section

5.1
, we use insights from the mappings to analyse the definition of causal influence in a tensor network proposed in
[
CHQY19
]
, and propose a slightly modified version of it in
Section

5.2
, called
operational causal influence
, with a clear operational interpretation in terms of conditional probabilities.

•

Connections between signalling and operational causal influence:
In
Section

5.3
, we show that the operational causal influence in a given tensor network is preserved as signalling in the image causal model given by our mappings, and vice versa. Thus, we build a concrete connection between these two properties that allows the study of one using tools known for the other. Moreover, this shows that the causal influence definition of
[
CHQY19
]
, up to the modification we propose, does in fact capture causal structure rather than mere correlations, in an operationally meaningful way.

•

Discrete rotations of causal models:
An interesting consequence of the mappings is explored in
Section

5.4
. The mappings show that from a given tensor network on a undirected graph one can define a set of genuinely distinct causal models by choosing different directions of edges in the undirected graph. While the connectivity of the directed graphs underlying such causal models differs from one to the other, they all share the same no-signalling relations. Thus, one can think of such class of causal models as space-time rotations of each other, in similar flavour to the concept of so-called
dual unitaries

[
PBCP20
]
.

•

Application to the holographic tensor network:
In
Section

6
, we apply the mappings to a specific tensor network modelling the holographic principle
[
PYHP15
]
. We show how known results involving properties of directed graphs, such as the
d
-separation theorem
[
Pea09
,
HLP14
]
, can be applied to an image causal model of the holographic tensor network to infer causal influence.

Finally,
Section

7
discusses future avenues of this work.

1.2
Notation

In the following, we denote with
\mathcal{H}
a finite-dimensional Hilbert space and with
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
the complex vector space of linear operators from
\mathcal{H}
to
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
, and denote
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}):=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H})
. When a Hilbert space is associated to a system
S
, we denote it as
\mathcal{H}_{S}
.
The normalised and un-normalised maximally entangled state on
\mathcal{H}_{A}\cong\mathcal{H}_{A^{\prime}}
are respectively denoted as

|\Phi^{+}\rangle_{AA^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{A}}}\sum_{i}|i\rangle_{A}\otimes|i\rangle_{A^{\prime}}\quad|\Omega^{+}\rangle_{AA^{\prime}}=\sum_{i}|i\rangle_{A}\otimes|i\rangle_{A^{\prime}}.

(1)

We denote a superoperator
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{A}),\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{B}))
, as
\mathcal{E}_{B|A}
. We denote sequential composition between superoperators
\mathcal{M}_{B|A}
and
\mathcal{N}_{C|B}
as
\mathcal{N}_{C|B}\circ\mathcal{M}_{B|A}\equiv\mathcal{T}_{C|A}
and keep factors of identity implicit, i.e.,
\mathcal{E}_{BD|A}
and
\mathcal{F}_{C|BE}
can be composed as
\mathcal{F}_{C|BE}\circ\mathcal{E}_{BD|A}\equiv\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{CD|AE}
(where an identity on the
E
system is implicit).
We use the acronyms: CP for completely positive, CPTP for completely positive and trace preserving, and POVM for positive operator valued measurement.

Graph notation

We denote with
\vec{G}=(\vec{V},\vec{E})
a directed graph, denoting directed edges as ordered pairs, i.e.,
e=(v,w)\in\vec{E}
.
The incoming and outgoing edges to a vertex
v\in\vec{V}
are denoted as

\begin{split}\mathrm{In}(v)&=\big\{e\in\vec{E}\,\big|\,\exists v^{\prime}\in\vec{V}:\ e=(v^{\prime},v)\big\},\\
\mathrm{Out}(v)&=\big\{e\in\vec{E}\,\big|\,\exists v^{\prime}\in\vec{V}:\ e=(v,v^{\prime})\big\},\end{split}

(2)

while the parents and children of a vertex
v\in\vec{V}
are denoted

\begin{split}\mathrm{Pa}(v)&=\big\{v^{\prime}\in\vec{V}\,\big|\,(v^{\prime},v)\in\vec{E}\big\},\\
\mathrm{Ch}(v)&=\big\{v^{\prime}\in\vec{V}\,\big|\,(v,v^{\prime})\in\vec{E}\big\}.\end{split}

(3)

We denote with
\bar{G}=(\bar{V},\bar{E})
an undirected graph, and undirected edges as two-element sets, i.e.,
e=\{v,w\}\in\bar{E}
, highlighting the absence of any ordering between elements of such pairs. The incident (or adjacent) edges to a vertex
v\in\bar{V}
are denoted

\mathrm{Adj}(v)=\big\{e\in\bar{E}\,\big|\,v\in e\big\}.

(4)

We consider graphs, both directed and undirected, with no open edges, i.e., edges are always connecting two vertices.

Choi-Jamiołkowski isomorphism

The Choi-Jamiołkowski isomorphism
[
Cho75
,
Jam72
]
connects linear superoperators in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{A}),\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{B}))
to linear operators in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{A^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})
where
\mathcal{H}_{A}\cong\mathcal{H}_{A^{\prime}}
.

We denote the direct Choi-Jamiołkowski mapping as
\mathbb{J}_{B|A}:\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{A}),\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{B})\right)\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{A^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})
, and define it as

\mathbb{J}_{B|A}(\mathcal{E}_{B|A})=(\mathcal{E}_{B|A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{A^{\prime}})\left(|\Phi^{+}\rangle\langle\Phi^{+}|_{AA^{\prime}}\right).

(5)

We denote the inverse as
\mathbb{J}^{-1}_{B|A}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{A^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{A}),\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{B}))
. One can prove that the inverse of
equation

5
acts on
S_{A}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
as

\mathbb{J}^{-1}_{B|A}(M_{A^{\prime}B})(S_{A})=d_{A}\mathrm{Tr}_{A^{\prime}}\left(\mathcal{T}_{A^{\prime}|A}(S_{A})M_{A^{\prime}B}\right)

(6)

where

\mathcal{T}_{A^{\prime}|A}(S_{A})=\sum_{i,j}|i\rangle\langle j|_{A^{\prime}}\langle j|S_{A}|i\rangle_{A}.

(7)

We recall the following result characterising CPTP maps.

Lemma 1
.

A superoperator
M_{B|A}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{A}\right),\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{B}\right)\right)
is
completely positive
if and only if
\mathbb{J}_{B|A}(M_{B|A})
is positive and it is
trace preserving
if and only if
\mathrm{Tr}_{B}\left[\mathbb{J}_{B|A}(M_{A|B})\right]=\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{A}}{d_{A}}
.

Proof.

See Section 5.3 of
[
Ren22
]
.
∎

Further, we introduce the pure inverse Choi-Jamiołkowski isomorphism, denoted as
\mathbb{J}^{-1,\textup{pure}}_{B|A}:\mathcal{H}_{A^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{A},\mathcal{H}_{B})
, defined as

\mathbb{J}^{-1,\textup{pure}}_{B|A}(|\Psi\rangle_{A^{\prime}B})(|\phi\rangle_{A})=\langle\Phi^{+}|_{AA^{\prime}}(|\psi\rangle_{A}\otimes|\Psi\rangle_{A^{\prime}B})\in\mathcal{H}_{B}.

(8)

Notice that
\mathbb{J}^{-1,\textup{pure}}_{B|A}(|\Psi\rangle_{A^{\prime}B})=\langle\Phi^{+}|_{AA^{\prime}}|\Psi\rangle_{A^{\prime}B}
is then a linear transformation from
\mathcal{H}_{A}
to
\mathcal{H}_{B}
. The following lemma connects the inverse to the pure inverse Choi-Jamiołkowski.

Lemma 2
.

Suppose that
M_{A^{\prime}B}=\sum_{k}p_{k}|\Psi_{k}\rangle\langle\Psi_{k}|_{A^{\prime}B}
where each
|\Psi_{k}\rangle
is normalised. Then,

\mathbb{J}^{-1}_{B|A}(M_{A^{\prime}B})=d_{A}^{2}\sum_{k}p_{k}\mathbb{J}^{-1,\textup{pure}}_{B|A}(|\Psi_{k}\rangle_{A^{\prime}B})\otimes\Big(\mathbb{J}^{-1,\textup{pure}}_{B|A}(|\Psi_{k}\rangle_{A^{\prime}B})\Big)^{\dagger}.

(9)

Proof.

See
Appendix

A
.
∎

2
Tensor networks and causal influence

Tensor networks are a versatile representation of tensors and sums of thereof which have found multiple applications in physics, e.g., in condensed matter, quantum information theory and quantum error correction, but also in data science and machine learning. Tensors, i.e., collections of complex numbers labelled by indices
T=\{T_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}
, are represented through shapes having as many legs as the tensor indices

(11)

In principle, any system or dynamics which can be described in terms of summation of tensors can be represented through a tensor network. For instance, quantum mechanical states and channels can be easily embedded in a tensor network up to choosing a preferred basis. As the notation portrays, all indices of tensors in a tensor network are of the same type, meaning that there is no such thing as “inputs” or “output” of a tensor. Therefore, tensor networks are agnostic of any direction, for instance of time, that could underlie the dynamics that they describe. This feature is crucial for studying the emergence of space-time from quantum correlations. Indeed, using tensor networks we can describe a quantum mechanical system and dynamics without assuming any predefined notion of time nor of cause and effect, and infer properties from the connectivity of the network only.

2.1
Definition of tensor network

We use a compact way of defining a tensor network used, for instance, in
[
CHQY19
]
.

Definition 1

(Tensor network)
.

A tensor network is specified by

1.

an undirected graph
\bar{G}=(\bar{V},\bar{E})
;

2.

a finite dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{e}
associated to each edge
e\in\bar{E}
;
1
1

1

We use a notation where

\mathcal{H}_{E^{\prime}}=\bigotimes_{e\in E^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}_{e}
for
E^{\prime}\subseteq\bar{E}
.

3.

a density operator
P_{v}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Adj}(v)}\right)
associated to each vertex
v\in\bar{V}
.

We denote the triple defining a tensor network as
\mathsf{Tn}=\left(\bar{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\bar{E}},\{P_{v}\}_{v\in\bar{V}}\right)
.

For our results, it is sufficient to allow the operators specified in
3.
to be un-normalised positive semi-definite operators; in
Remark

1
, we clarify how any tensor network defined in terms of coefficients as above can be written in this form. Notice that tensors with open legs are not allowed by
Definition

1
.

We define the
link state
and
total state
of a given tensor network, which allow us to compute correlation functions of the network.

Definition 2

(Link state and total state)
.

Let
\mathsf{Tn}=(\bar{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\bar{E}},\{P_{v}\}_{v\in\bar{V}})
be a tensor network. For each
e=\{v,w\}\in\bar{E}
, let us introduce two Hilbert spaces denoted as
\mathcal{H}_{(e,v)}
and
\mathcal{H}_{(e,w)}
, where
\mathcal{H}_{(e,v)}\cong\mathcal{H}_{(e,w)}\cong\mathcal{H}_{e}
and denote
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{tot}}=\bigotimes_{v\in\bar{V}}\bigotimes_{e\in\mathrm{Adj}(v)}\mathcal{H}_{(e,v)}\cong\mathcal{H}_{\bar{E}}^{\otimes 2}
. We define

•

the link state
|L\rangle\in\mathcal{H}_{\textup{tot}}
as

|L\rangle=\bigotimes_{e\in\bar{E}}|\Omega^{+}\rangle_{e}

(12)

where for
e=\{v,w\}
, the state
|\Omega^{+}\rangle_{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{(e,v)}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{(e,w)}
is the un-normalised maximally entangled state;

•

the total state
\rho_{P}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\textup{tot}}\right)

\rho_{P}=\bigotimes_{v\in\bar{V}}P_{v}

(13)

and
P_{v}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\otimes_{e\in\mathrm{Adj}(v)}\mathcal{H}_{(e,v)}\right)
.

Notice that a tensor network is originally defined through associating a Hilbert space to each edge, i.e.,
\mathcal{H}_{e}
to
e\in\bar{E}
. However, in the former definition each edge is then associated with two Hilbert spaces isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}_{e}
so that one can define a maximally entangled state over them. This doubling ensures that no direction of the edge is implicitly induced in defining the tensor contraction below.

We now define the tensor contraction which allows to compute correlation functions of a tensor network.

Definition 3

(Tensor contraction)
.

Let
\mathsf{Tn}=(\bar{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\bar{E}},\{P_{v}\}_{v\in\bar{V}})
be a tensor network. The tensor contraction is given by

\langle L|\rho_{P}|L\rangle\in\mathbb{R}.

(14)

For any two linear operators
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\textup{tot}}\right)
, we define the corresponding correlation function as

\langle L|Q_{1}\rho_{P}Q_{2}|L\rangle\in\mathbb{C}.

(15)

An explicit computation of the tensor contraction is provided in the example below.

Remark 1
.

While tensors are generally defined as a collection of numbers, in
Definition

1
a tensor is instead specified by a density operator. We now clarify how one can embed a tensor into a possibly un-normalised density operator and argue that the normalization can be appropriately neglected when studying observable properties of the network. Consider a tensor
T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}
for
i_{k}=1,\dots,d_{k}
, and define the vector

|\psi_{T}\rangle=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}^{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}}T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}|i_{1}\rangle\otimes\dots\otimes|i_{n}\rangle

(16)

in a Hilbert space
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{H}_{j}
where
\dim(\mathcal{H}_{j})=d_{j}
. Then, the operator associated to the tensor
T
is
\rho_{T}=|\psi_{T}\rangle\langle\psi_{T}|
, which, up to a real and positive constant representing the normalisation of the state, is a density operator.
Hence, to an arbitrary collection of numbers, we can always associate a density operator up to a real and positive constant. Such a constant only acts as a global scaling of the tensor, which affects all correlation functions equally. Thus, for the study of correlation functions, one can without loss of generality set such a constant to be one. In our case, the mappings formulated in
Section

4
also apply to un-normalised density matrices without fixing this constant to one, and the definitions of causal influence remain well defined, since they involve a renormalisation (see
Definitions

4
and
17
).

(17)

with associated states

|\psi_{c}\rangle_{1}=\sum_{i}c_{i}|i\rangle_{1},\quad|\psi_{M}\rangle_{2,3}=\sum_{i,j}M_{ij}|i\rangle_{2}|j\rangle_{3},

(18)

where

are system labels.
Through the inner product with a maximally entangled state, we obtain

\langle\Omega^{+}|_{12}|\psi_{c}\rangle_{1}\otimes|\psi_{M}\rangle_{2,3}=\sum_{i,j}c_{i}M_{ij}|j\rangle_{3}\equiv|\psi_{\tilde{c}}\rangle_{3}.

(19)

(20)

We obtain

\langle\Omega^{+}|_{3,4}|\psi_{\tilde{c}}\rangle_{3}\otimes|\psi_{b}\rangle_{4}=\sum_{i,j}c_{i}M_{ij}b_{j}

(21)

corresponding to the tensor summation.

As a consequence of this way of embedding tensors into pure states, we remark that the tensor network contraction of
Definition

3
equals the square of the tensor summation, a quantity which is used to compute correlation functions. For more details we refer to
[
CHQY19
]
.

Using the compact notation of
Definition

1
, the example above is equivalently defined by:

1.

the undirected graph

v_{c}
v_{M}
v_{b}

, with vertex set
\bar{V}=\{v_{c},v_{M},v_{b}\}
and edge set
\bar{E}=\{\{v_{M},v_{c}\}\equiv e_{1},\{v_{M},v_{b}\}\equiv e_{2}\}
;

2.

Hilbert spaces associated to
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
respectively with dimensions
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{H}_{1}=\textup{span}\{|i\rangle\}_{i=1}^{d_{1}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}=\textup{span}\{|j\rangle\}_{j=1}^{d_{2}}
;

3.

the following linear operators

P_{c}=|\psi_{c}\rangle\langle\psi_{c}|_{1},\quad P_{M}=|\psi_{M}\rangle\langle\psi_{M}|_{1,2},\quad P_{b}=|\psi_{b}\rangle\langle\psi_{b}|_{2}.

(22)

Notice that the undirected graph used to define a tensor network is the same underlying the network itself in the rightmost diagram in
equation

20
.

To define the link state and total state let us define isomorphic Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{1_{c}}\cong\mathcal{H}_{1_{M}}\cong\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2_{b}}\cong\mathcal{H}_{2_{M}}\cong\mathcal{H}_{2}
. Then we define

\rho_{P}=\underbrace{|\psi_{c}\rangle\langle\psi_{c}|_{1_{c}}}_{P_{c}}\otimes\underbrace{|\psi_{b}\rangle\langle\psi_{b}|_{2_{b}}}_{P_{b}}\otimes\underbrace{|\psi_{M}\rangle\langle\psi_{M}|_{1_{M},2_{M}}}_{P_{M}}

(23)

and

|L\rangle=|\Omega^{+}\rangle_{1_{c},1_{M}}\otimes|\Omega^{+}\rangle_{2_{b},2_{M}}=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{1}}|i\rangle_{1_{c}}\otimes|i\rangle_{1_{M}}\otimes\sum_{j=1}^{b_{2}}|j\rangle_{2_{b}}\otimes|j\rangle_{2_{M}}.

(24)

Then we evaluate the tensor contraction as:

\begin{split}&\langle L|\rho_{P}|L\rangle\\
&=\langle\Omega^{+}|_{1_{c},1_{M}}\otimes\langle\Omega^{+}|_{2_{b},2_{M}}\left(|\psi_{c}\rangle\langle\psi_{c}|_{1_{c}}\otimes|\psi_{b}\rangle\langle\psi_{b}|_{2_{b}}\otimes|\psi_{M}\rangle\langle\psi_{M}|_{1_{M},2_{M}}\right)|\Omega^{+}\rangle_{1_{c},1_{M}}\otimes|\Omega^{+}\rangle_{2_{b},2_{M}}\\
&=\left|\sum_{ij=1}^{d_{1},d_{2}}c_{i}M_{ij}b_{j}\right|^{2},\end{split}

(25)

thus showing that we obtain the square of the tensor summation in
equation

21
.

2.2
Causal influence as defined in
[
CHQY19
]

In
[
CHQY19
]
, a definition of causal influence in tensor networks is proposed. Given two disjoint subsets of edges of the network,
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, one says that there is causal influence from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
if correlations between a Hermitian observable inserted in
\mathcal{B}
and a unitary operation applied in
\mathcal{A}
depend on which unitary is chosen in
\mathcal{A}
. In other words, causal relations are inferred by probing how local changes in one region of the network affect correlation functions in another.

Definition 4

(Quantum causal influence —
[
CHQY19
]
)
.

Let
\mathsf{Tn}=\left(\bar{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\bar{E}},\{P_{v}\}_{v\in\bar{V}}\right)
be a tensor network and consider an ordered pair of disjoint subsets of edges, i.e.,
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
such that
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\subseteq\bar{E}
and
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}=\emptyset
, which we call
regions
. Define the correlation function

M_{\textup{CHQY}}(U_{\mathcal{A}}:O_{\mathcal{B}})=\frac{\langle L|(U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes O_{\mathcal{B}})\rho_{P}(U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\dagger}\otimes O_{\mathcal{B}}^{\dagger})|L\rangle}{\langle L|(U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{B}})\rho_{P}(U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{B}})|L\rangle},

(26)

where
U_{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})
is a unitary and
O_{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}})
an Hermitian operator such that
O_{\mathcal{B}}^{\dagger}O_{\mathcal{B}}\leq\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{B}}
.
The quantum causal influence from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
is zero if and only if

M_{\textup{CHQY}}(U_{\mathcal{A}}:O_{\mathcal{B}})=M_{\textup{CHQY}}(U^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}:O_{\mathcal{B}})

(27)

for all
U_{\mathcal{A}}
and
U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
unitaries and for all
O_{\mathcal{B}}
Hermitian operators.

As discussed in the remark of the previous section, notice that eventual normalization factors of the operators
P_{v}
do not play any role in
Definition

4
because of the denominator.

Example

Let us study causal influence in the simple tensor network that we used as an example in the previous section (
equation

20
):

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{19.19374pt}{37.98055pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{{{}}}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-26.7354pt}{38.0361pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}
{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.87 7.83 L -23.65 31.54}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}
{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.74 39.37 L -19.74 39.37}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\ =\sum_{i,j}c_{i}M_{ij}b_{j},

(28)

and let
\mathcal{A}=\{e_{1}=\{v_{M},v_{c}\}\}
and
\mathcal{B}=\{e_{2}=\{v_{M},v_{b}\}\}
. Then, the causal influence involves the study of correlation functions

\begin{split}&\langle L|(U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes O_{\mathcal{B}})\rho_{P}(U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\dagger}\otimes O_{\mathcal{B}}^{\dagger})|L\rangle\\
&=\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{d_{1},d_{2}}\langle i|_{1_{c}}\otimes\langle i|_{1_{M}}\otimes\langle j|_{2_{b}}\otimes\langle j|_{2_{M}}\right)\left(U_{1_{c}}\otimes O_{2_{b}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{1_{M},2_{M}}\right)\\
&\quad\left(|\psi_{c}\rangle\langle\psi_{c}|_{1_{c}}\otimes|\psi_{b}\rangle\langle\psi_{b}|_{2_{b}}\otimes|\psi_{M}\rangle\langle\psi_{M}|_{1_{M},2_{M}}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\left(U^{\dagger}_{1_{c}}\otimes O^{\dagger}_{2_{b}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{1_{M},2_{M}}\right)\left(\sum_{k,l=1}^{d_{1},d_{2}}|k\rangle_{1_{c}}\otimes|k\rangle_{1_{M}}\otimes|l\rangle_{2_{b}}\otimes|l\rangle_{2_{M}}\right).\end{split}

(29)

The Hilbert spaces associated to each edge are doubled in defining the total state and link state. This creates an ambiguity in
Definition

4
for the spaces on which the unitary and Hermitian operators act on the doubled Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{tot}}
. In the next section, we argue that for determining whether there is causal influence or not, one can choose arbitrarily on which space to apply the operation because of the structure of the link state.

2.3
Remarks and open questions regarding this notion causal influence

The notion of causal influence of
Definition

4
is extensively motivated in
[
CHQY19
]
.
Here we summarize the main arguments clarifying the definition and we collect open questions regarding it.
In
Section

5.1
, we will continue the discussion on causal influence and motivate the introduction of a modified version of it.

On which Hilbert spaces are the operators
U_{\mathcal{A}}
and
O_{\mathcal{B}}
acting?

The doubling of Hilbert spaces, necessary to define the total state and link state, leads to an ambiguity on where the operators
U_{\mathcal{A}}
and
O_{\mathcal{B}}
act. Indeed, the total and link states are defined on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{tot}}=\bigotimes_{v\in\bar{V}}\bigotimes_{e\in\mathrm{Adj}(v)}\mathcal{H}_{(e,v)}\cong\mathcal{H}_{\bar{E}}^{\otimes 2}
thus there are two subspaces isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
and two subspaces isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
.
Definition

4
does not specify on which of these spaces the unitary and Hermitian operator act on. However, for determining whether there is causal influence or not one can arbitrarily choose on which space to apply the unitary as long as the choice is consistent in all evaluations of the quantity
M
for different unitaries and Hermitian operators. More details, including a proof of this statement, can be found in
Appendix

B
.

Why are unitaries and Hermitian operators introduced?

Tensor networks do not have a predefined direction, in particular no predefined notion of input and output. This property should be preserved when modifying the network to test information flow. In particular, the regions in which we introduce modifications correspond to undirected edges. Therefore, any operation inserted in these regions must be implemented in a way that does not impose an artificial direction on those edges. For this reason, the authors propose modifications of the network that are unitary operators, which remain unitary if we reverse input and output, and Hermitian operators which are invariant up to reversing inputs and outputs.

What is the role of the denominator?

We aim to quantify the influence from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
in the tensor network. This amounts to modifying locally the network in
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
and evaluate correlation functions to quantify how modifications in
\mathcal{A}
affect the network in
\mathcal{B}
. However, it might be that a modification in
\mathcal{A}
, generally affects the network contraction but not necessarily in
\mathcal{B}
. Explicitly, it might be that for two unitaries
U_{\mathcal{A}}
and
V_{\mathcal{A}}

\langle L|(U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{B}})\rho_{P}(U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{B}})|L\rangle\neq\langle L|(V_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{B}})\rho_{P}(V_{\mathcal{A}}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{B}})|L\rangle.

(30)

The role of the denominator is to normalize the network contraction so that the measure
M_{\textup{CHQY}}
solely quantifies the influence in
\mathcal{B}
and not in the global network contraction. In addition, the denominator ensures that correlations functions are not affected by possible normalisation factors that one introduces to describe a given tensor as a density operator.

Open questions

Definition

4
provides a notion of causality in a tensor network based on correlation functions. This naturally raises questions concerning both its interpretation and its relation to existing approaches to causality.

A first question is whether and how this notion can be connected to causal modelling frameworks such as
[
FGV25b
,
BLO21
,
HLP14
]
. These frameworks typically assume a predefined distinction of inputs and outputs of their building blocks, and within them several techniques for analysing causal structure are known. Establishing such a connection could therefore allow techniques developed in one formalism to be transferred to the other.

A second, closely related question concerns the interpretation of defining causal influence purely in terms of correlation functions: it is not immediately clear to what extent
Definition

4
captures genuine causation rather than mere correlations. This issue is fundamental, as distinguishing causation from correlation is one of the main motivations behind causal modelling frameworks, where this distinction is addressed in a formal and rigorous way, typically through the notion of interventions.

Moreover, although the role of the denominator in
Definition

4
is motivated as described above, it is not evident that its particular form fully accomplishes this purpose. In the absence of a clear operational interpretation, it remains ambiguous whether the normalization indeed isolates causal influence rather than simply rescaling correlations. In
Section

5.2
, we propose a modified definition which replaces the denominator with a term that admits a direct operational meaning in terms of conditional probabilities of outcomes under freely chosen interventions. This ensures that the resulting quantity captures genuine causal effects rather than mere statistical correlations.

Finally, these considerations point to the need for a clearer operational interpretation of causal influence in tensor networks. In particular, one would like to understand why the proposed definition should be regarded as describing causality and how it relates to operational procedures.
As we will see in
Sections

5.1
and
5.2
, these questions lead to a modified version of
Definition

4
, which can be connected to known notions of causality and, through this connection, provides tensor networks with a clearer operational meaning.

3
Cyclic causal models and signalling

In this section, we briefly review the causal modelling framework presented in
[
FGV25b
]
. This framework allows modelling of finite dimensional causal models on cyclic graphs, through formulating a probability rule which is well-defined in all but a handful of pathological models.
Different quantum causal modelling frameworks have been proposed
[
BLO20
,
HLP14
]
, however, these do not allow for arbitrary models on generally cyclic graphs. As we will see in the following sections, tensor networks, which do not have a predefined direction of time or cause-and-effect, can generally lead to cyclic causal explanation. Thus, a generally cyclic framework is necessary to accommodate this possibility.

In what follows, we first introduce the notation and definition of a causal model, and present the probability rule. We refer to
[
FGV25b
]
for all the proofs and additional results related to the framework. Then, we introduce the notion of interventions and define signalling for these causal models. Among others, the topics of this section are the content of a follow-up work focussed on intervention in cyclic scenarios
[
FV
]
.

3.1
Causal modelling framework

Causal models rely on a graphical representation of cause and effect relations through so-called
causal graphs
.

Definition 5

(Causal graph)
.

A causal graph is a directed graph
\vec{G}=(\vec{V},\vec{E})
such that
\vec{V}
can be partitioned into
\vec{V}=V_{\textup{o}}\cup V_{\textup{u}}
, where
v\in V_{\textup{o}}
are called the
observed
vertices, denoted as

v

, and
v\in V_{\textup{u}}
are called the
unobserved
vertices and are denoted as

v

.

A causal model assigns to a causal graph specific causal mechanisms for each vertex.
These causal mechanisms have to match the type of the vertex, e.g., if the vertex is observed, the causal mechanism has to specify how the observed outcome is obtained.

Definition 6

(Causal model on causal graph)
.

A causal model on a causal graph is specified by the following items:

1.

a causal graph
\vec{G}=(\vec{V},\vec{E})
;

2.

a finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{e}
is associated to each edge
e\in\vec{E}
;
2
2

2

We use a notation where

\mathcal{H}_{E^{\prime}}=\bigotimes_{e\in E^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}_{e}
for
E^{\prime}\subseteq\vec{E}
.

3.

a quantum operation
\mathcal{T}^{v}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{In}(v)}),\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Out}(v)}))
associated to each vertex
v\in\vec{V}
as follows:

•

for unobserved
v\in V_{\textup{u}}
a single CPTP map
\mathcal{T}^{v}=\{\mathcal{E}^{v}\}
,

•

for observed
v\in V_{\textup{o}}
an instrument
\mathcal{T}^{v}=\{\mathcal{M}_{x}^{v}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{v}}
, i.e., a collection of CP maps, each acting on
\rho\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{In}(v)})
as

\mathcal{M}_{x}^{v}(\rho)=\mathrm{Tr}[E_{x}^{v}\rho]\bigotimes_{e\in\mathrm{Out}(v)}|x\rangle\langle x|_{e}

(31)

where
\left\{E_{x}^{v}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{In}(v))\right\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{v}}
is a POVM associated with a random variable
X_{v}
taking values from a finite set
x_{v}\in\mathcal{X}_{v}
. We denote the CPTP map obtained through marginalisation as
\mathcal{E}^{v}=\sum_{x}\mathcal{M}_{x}^{v}

We denote the triple defining a causal model as
\mathsf{Cm}=(\vec{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\vec{E}},\{\mathcal{T}^{v}\}_{v\in\vec{V}})
.

The definition above is a simplified version of definition 2 in
[
FGV25b
]
phrased to highlight the analogy with
Definition

1
of a tensor network.
Definition

6
retains all the defining features of a causal model which are needed here, while neglecting features which do not play any role in what follows. For instance, the dimensions of Hilbert spaces associated to outgoing edges of observed vertices have to match the cardinality of the finite set associated to the vertex. Notice that for an exogenous vertex
v
, the associated maps are elements of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}),\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Out}(v)}))\cong\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C},\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Out}(v)}))\cong\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Out}(v)})
. We refer to definition 2 from
[
FGV25b
]
for more details.

We also note that there exist several distinct frameworks of quantum causal models in the literature, differing in their underlying assumptions, mathematical frameworks, and regime of validity. The relation between the present framework and alternative approaches is discussed in detail in
[
FGV25b
]
. Conceptually, the definition adopted here can be viewed as a cyclic extension of the causal models introduced in
[
HLP14
]
, restricted to quantum theory.
3
3

3

[
HLP14
]
defined a causal modelling approach for acyclic graphs, but applicable to a general class of operational probabilistic theories, including quantum theory.

3.2
Probability rule

Causal modelling formalisms include a prescription of how to evaluate probabilities over the outcomes associated to observed vertices of a causal graph, using the specified causal mechanisms (here, CP/CPTP maps, POVMs etc).
Within the framework of
[
FGV25b
]
, a probability rule valid for arbitrary finite-dimensional causal models, i.e., those given by
Definition

6
, is provided. For a complete analysis of the probability rule we refer to section 4 of
[
FGV25b
]
.

Here, we present a formulation of the probability rule in
[
FGV25b
]
which relies on a special type of channel composition called
self-cycle composition
. This formulation is equivalent to the probability rule of
[
FGV25b
]
(see
Appendix

C
).

Firstly, we introduce the
self-cycle composition
for quantum channels
[
VR24a
]
, which, in the finite-dimensional setting considered here, is equivalent to loop composition presented in
[
PMM+17
]
.

Definition 7

(Self-cycle composition)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{A}\cong\mathcal{H}_{C}
with
d=\dim(\mathcal{H}_{A})=\dim(\mathcal{H}_{C})
. Further, let
\{|k\rangle_{A}\}_{k=1}^{d}
and
\{|l\rangle_{A}\}_{l=1}^{d}
be any orthonormal bases of
\mathcal{H}_{A}
and
\{|k\rangle_{C}\}_{k=1}^{d}
and
\{|l\rangle_{C}\}_{l=1}^{d}
be the corresponding bases of
\mathcal{H}_{C}
, i.e.,
|k\rangle_{A}\cong|k\rangle_{C}
and
|l\rangle_{A}\cong|l\rangle_{C}
for all
k,l=1,\dots,d
.
Then for any linear map
\mathcal{M}_{AB|CD}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{C}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{D})\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})
, we define the self-cycle composition
\mathrm{cycle}_{A,C}(\mathcal{M}_{AB|CD}):\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{D})\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{B})
as follows

\displaystyle\mathrm{cycle}_{A,C}(\mathcal{M}_{AB|CD})=\sum_{k,l=1}^{d}\langle k|_{A}\mathcal{M}_{AB|CD}(|k\rangle\langle l|_{C})|l\rangle_{A}.

(32)

It is worth noting that completely positive maps are closed under loop composition, whereas completely positive trace-preserving maps are not: composing a CPTP map with a loop may yield a map that remains CP but is no longer trace-preserving.

Notice that if the spaces
\mathcal{H}_{B}
and
\mathcal{H}_{D}
are trivial,
\mathrm{cycle}_{A,C}(\mathcal{M}_{A|C})\in\mathbb{C}
. In this case, we will omit the subscript and denote the composition as
\mathrm{cycle}(\mathcal{M}_{A|C})
. In addition, we might sometimes use the same label for the isomorphic systems
A
and
C
, and denote the composition as
\mathrm{cycle}_{A}
.

Let us define a collection of CP maps which compactly contains the maps associated to vertices of a given causal model.

Definition 8

(Total maps of a causal model)
.

Let
\mathsf{Cm}=(\vec{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\vec{E}},\{\mathcal{T}^{v}\}_{v\in\vec{V}})
be a causal model, whose causal graph has sets
V_{\textup{o}}
and
V_{\textup{u}}
of observed and unobserved vertices.
We denote a joint observed event associated with
V_{\textup{o}}
as
x:=\{x_{v}\in\mathcal{X}_{v}\}_{v\in V_{\textup{o}}}
, and, introducing
\mathcal{H}_{\vec{E}_{\textup{in}}}\cong\mathcal{H}_{\vec{E}_{\textup{out}}}\cong\mathcal{H}_{\vec{E}}
, define the collection of CP maps, called total maps,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{C}}=\Big\{\mathcal{C}^{x}:\textstyle\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\vec{E}_{\textup{in}}}\right)\mapsto\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\vec{E}_{\textup{out}}}\right)\Big\}_{x}

(33)

through

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{x}=\bigotimes_{v\in V_{\textup{o}}}\mathcal{M}_{x_{v}}^{v}\bigotimes_{v^{\prime}\in V_{\textup{u}}}\mathcal{E}^{v^{\prime}},

(34)

where
\mathcal{M}_{x_{v}}^{v}
and
\mathcal{E}^{v^{\prime}}
refer to the maps of
\mathsf{Cm}
(see
Definition

6
). Notice that by
Definition

6
,
\mathcal{C}^{x}
has input in
\mathcal{H}_{\vec{E}_{\textup{in}}}=\cup_{v\in V}\mathrm{In}(v)
and output in
\mathcal{H}_{\vec{E}_{\textup{out}}}=\cup_{v\in V}\mathrm{Out}(v)
, and both spaces are isomorphic to the space
\mathcal{H}_{\vec{E}}
. Furthermore, we will refer to
\mathcal{C}:=\sum_{x}\mathcal{C}^{x}
as the marginalised total map of
\mathsf{Cm}
.

Now, we define the probability rule through self-cycle composition of the total map of a causal model as follows.

Definition 9

(Probability distribution of a causal model)
.

Consider a causal model
\mathsf{Cm}=(\vec{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\vec{E}},\{\mathcal{T}^{v}\}_{v\in\vec{V}})
and let a joint observed event associated with
V_{\textup{o}}
be denoted as
x:=\{x_{v}\in\mathcal{X}_{v}\}_{v\in V_{\textup{o}}}
.
If
\sum_{x}\mathrm{cycle}(\mathcal{C}^{x})\neq 0
, the probability
\mathrm{Pr}(x)_{\vec{G}}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(x)_{\vec{G}}=\frac{\mathrm{cycle}(\mathcal{C}^{x})}{\sum_{x}\mathrm{cycle}(\mathcal{C}^{x})}.

(35)

If
\sum_{x}\mathrm{cycle}(\mathcal{C}^{x})=0
, we say that the causal model is inconsistent and the probabilities
\mathrm{Pr}\left(x\right)_{\vec{G}}
are undefined.

In
Appendix

C
, we recall results from
[
FGV25b
]
showing that the probability rule of
Definition

9
can be equivalently obtained using the acyclic probability rule and post-selection. The denominator appearing in
Definition

9
is related to the success probability of the post-sections.
As shown in
[
FGV25b
]
, in case the causal graph is acyclic, the denominator is
1
and the numerator is the usual sequential composition of channels which leads to the Born rule. For (possibly cyclic) causal models associated to valid process matrices
[
OCB12
,
BLO21
]
, the denominator is also
1
and the numerator gives the generalised Born rule defined for processes
[
FGV25b
]
. Thus,
Definition

9
recovers known probability rules in existing frameworks.
For more details we refer to sections
4
,
6
and
7
of
[
FGV25b
]
.

For connecting tensor networks to causal models we need the framework in
[
FGV25b
]
, as the absence of a predefined causal direction generally leads to cycles. Hence, building up the connection within our framework ensures that such possibility is not a priori ruled out.

Example

Consider the following directed graph as an example

\vec{G}=\ \vbox{\hbox{\hbox to165.42pt{\vbox to77.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 82.7069pt\lower-38.96039pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{-54.91931pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.22 0 L 0.69 2.67 L 0 0 L 0.69 -2.67 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-54.36353pt}{5.33301pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\ .

(36)

The edges are labelled as
e_{1}=(v_{3},v_{1})
,
e_{2}=(v_{4},v_{2})
,
e_{3}=(v_{3},v_{4})
and
e_{4}=(v_{4},v_{3})
. We define a causal model by first associating finite dimensional Hilbert spaces to each edge:
\mathcal{H}_{i}
to
e_{i}
. Then, we associate CPTP maps

\mathcal{E}^{3}:\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{4}\right)\mapsto\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{3}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{1}\right)
to
v_{3}
and
\mathcal{E}^{4}:\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{3}\right)\mapsto\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{4}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}\right)
to
v_{4}
. To the observed vertices,
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, we associate collections of CP maps acting on
\rho_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{1})
and
\rho_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{2})
, as

\mathcal{M}_{x_{1}}^{(1)}(\rho_{1})=\mathrm{Tr}(E^{(1)}_{x_{1}}\rho_{1})\textup{ and }\mathcal{M}_{x_{2}}^{(2)}(\rho_{2})=\mathrm{Tr}(E^{(2)}_{x_{2}}\rho_{2})

(37)

where

E^{(1)}=\left\{E_{x_{1}}^{(1)}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{1}\right)\right\}_{x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}}\textup{ and }E^{(2)}=\left\{E_{x_{2}}^{(2)}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{2}\right)\right\}_{x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}}

(38)

are POVMs, and
\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}
are finite sets.

The total map associated to the observed event
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}
is

\mathcal{C}^{x_{1},x_{2}}=\mathcal{M}_{x_{1}}^{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{x_{2}}^{(2)}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{3}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{4}

(39)

The probability of a joint observed event associated with outcomes
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, is

\mathrm{Pr}(x_{1},x_{2})_{\vec{G}}=\frac{\mathrm{cycle}\left(\mathcal{C}^{x_{1},x_{2}}\right)}{\sum_{y_{1},y_{2}}\mathrm{cycle}\left(\mathcal{C}^{y_{1},y_{2}}\right)}.

(40)

3.3
Interventions and signalling

Signalling refers to the possibility for one party (or agent),
\mathbbm{A}
, to transmit information to another,
\mathbbm{B}
, by means of their interventions on physical systems. In the language of our causal models, these physical systems are represented by the edges of the graph, and signalling between
\mathbbm{A}
and
\mathbbm{B}
corresponds to information being transmitted along the subset of systems (edges) that link them. In the causal modelling literature, signalling is probed through so-called
interventions
. These are modification of the causal model at hand, which introduce a set of possible operations on the edges associated to
\mathbbm{A}
and an observable on the edges associated to
\mathbbm{B}
.

Definition 10

(Intervention)
.

Let
\mathsf{Cm}=(\vec{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\vec{E}},\{\mathcal{T}^{v}\}_{v\in\vec{V}})
be a causal model and consider an ordered pair of disjoint subsets of edges, i.e.,
(\mathbbm{A},\mathbbm{B})
such that
\mathbbm{A},\mathbbm{B}\subseteq\vec{E}
and
\mathbbm{A}\cap\mathbbm{B}=\emptyset
, which we call
labs
. An intervention is defined by:

•

a collection of CPTP maps labelled by
a\in\mathcal{X}_{A}

\mathcal{E}_{\mathbbm{A}}=\left\{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbbm{A}}^{a}:\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbbm{A}}\right)\mapsto\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbbm{A}}\right)\right\}_{a\in\mathcal{X}_{A}}

(41)

associated to the lab
\mathbbm{A}
;

•

a collection of quantum instruments labelled by
b\in\mathcal{X}_{B}
and each having outcome
y\in\mathcal{X}_{Y}

\mathcal{M}_{\mathbbm{B}}=\left\{\left\{\mathcal{M}_{\mathbbm{B}}^{y|b}:\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbbm{B}}\right)\mapsto\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbbm{B}}\right)\right\}_{y\in\mathcal{X}_{Y}}\right\}_{b\in\mathcal{X}_{B}}

(42)

associated to the lab
\mathbbm{B}
.

Signalling is established by observing correlations between
\mathbbm{A}
’s freely chosen interventions and the observable outcomes at
\mathbbm{B}
. The freedom in
\mathbbm{A}
’s choice is essential: only correlations between such independent choices and
\mathbbm{B}
’s outcomes can witness signalling, thereby distinguishing genuine causal influence from mere pre-existing correlations. Thus, we define the statistics of an intervention through considering the joint probability of an observed event in the causal model and the outcome of the instrument in
\mathbbm{B}
, and signalling.

Definition 11

(Statistics of an intervention)
.

Let
\mathsf{Cm}=(\vec{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\vec{E}},\{\mathcal{T}^{v}\}_{v\in\vec{V}})
be a causal model, consider an intervention with the ordered pair
(\mathbbm{A},\mathbbm{B})
as labs and operations
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbbm{A}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbbm{B}}
. Let us denote a joint observed event associated with the vertices
V_{\textup{o}}
as
x:=\{x_{v}\in\mathcal{X}_{v}\}_{v\in V_{\textup{o}}}
, and let the total map of
\mathsf{Cm}
be

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{C}}=\Big\{\mathcal{C}^{x}:\textstyle\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{\vec{E}}\right)\mapsto\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\vec{E}}\right)\Big\}_{x}

(43)

where
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{\vec{E}}\cong\mathcal{H}_{\vec{E}}
.
Then, if
\sum_{x,y}\mathrm{cycle}\left(\left(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbbm{B}}^{y|b}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{\mathbbm{A}}^{a}\right)\circ\mathcal{C}^{x}\right)\neq 0
, the statistics of the intervention labelled by
(a,b)\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbbm{A}}\times\mathcal{X}_{\mathbbm{B}}
is defined as

\mathrm{Pr}(x,y|a,b)_{I}:=\frac{\mathrm{cycle}\left(\left(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbbm{B}}^{y|b}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{\mathbbm{A}}^{a}\right)\circ\mathcal{C}^{x}\right)}{\sum_{x,y}\mathrm{cycle}\left(\left(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbbm{B}}^{y|b}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{\mathbbm{A}}^{a}\right)\circ\mathcal{C}^{x}\right)}

(44)

otherwise we say that the intervention is inconsistent and the statistics is left undefined.

The above definition can be equivalently obtained through defining a causal model for each choice of intervention on
\mathbbm{A}
and
\mathbbm{B}
on a graph obtained from
\vec{G}
but with the addition of intervention vertices for
\mathbbm{A}
and
\mathbbm{B}
(see
equations

47
,
49
and
50
of the example below) and use the probability rule in
Definition

9
to evaluate the statistics of intervention. This procedure is described in a follow-up work in preparation
[
FV
]
.

Definition 12

(Signalling)
.

Let
\mathsf{Cm}=(\vec{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\vec{E}},\{\mathcal{T}^{v}\}_{v\in\vec{V}})
be a causal model, consider an intervention with the ordered pair
(\mathbbm{A},\mathbbm{B})
as labs and operations

\mathcal{E}_{\mathbbm{A}}=\left\{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbbm{A}}^{a}\right\}_{a\in\mathcal{X}_{A}}\textup{ and }\mathcal{M}_{\mathbbm{B}}=\left\{\left\{\mathcal{M}_{\mathbbm{B}}^{y|b}\right\}_{y\in\mathcal{X}_{Y}}\right\}_{b\in\mathcal{X}_{B}}.

(45)

We say that
\mathbbm{A}
signals to
\mathbbm{B}
in
\mathsf{Cm}
relative to the interventions
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbbm{A}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbbm{B}}
, if there exists
y\in\mathcal{X}_{Y}
,
b\in\mathcal{X}_{B}
and
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{A}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(y|a,b)_{I}\neq\mathrm{Pr}(y|a^{\prime},b)_{I}.

(46)

Example

Let us show how signalling can be studied in the cyclic example that we introduced before:

as unobserved (CPTP map) and
\mathbbm{B}
as observed (instruments):

(49)

One can easily convince themself that applying operations in
\mathbbm{A}
will in general affect the distribution in
\mathbbm{B}
, unless there is fine-tuning in the map associated to
v_{3}
. Differently, if we consider interventions where
\mathbbm{A}={e_{2}}
and
\mathbbm{B}={e_{1}}
, i.e.,

(50)

we see that modifications in
\mathbbm{A}
will at most affect the distribution in
v_{2}
but not the distribution in
\mathbbm{B}
.
The fact that we have no-signalling in this case is immediate from analysing graph separation properties (see
Appendix

D
) of

\vec{G}_{I_{1}}
. In particular, within the framework of
[
FGV25b
]
this follows directly from the notion of
p
-separation, which provides a systematic graphical criterion to infer conditional independencies—and hence no-signalling relations—from the structure of the intervention causal graph. For finite-dimensional quantum causal models on cyclic graphs,
p
-separation has been shown to be sound and complete
[
FGV25b
]
, meaning that whenever two vertices are
p
-separated given a third, the corresponding conditional independence holds in the observed probability distribution. In the present example, one can show that
\mathbbm{A}
and
\mathbbm{B}
are
p
-separated, and therefore conclude immediately that there is no-signalling from
\mathbbm{A}
to
\mathbbm{B}
. A more detailed analysis of interventions and the use of graph separation in quantum cyclic causal models will be presented in upcoming work focused specifically on this topic
[
FV
]
.

4
Mappings between tensor networks and cyclic causal models

Tensor networks and causal models, reviewed in the previous sections, are both graphical frameworks for describing relations between processes, but with different aims and assumptions. In this section, we connect them concretely by formulating mappings from one to the other.

4.1
Mapping causal models to tensor networks

Let us first formulate the mapping from a causal model to a unique tensor network.

Definition 13

(Mapping
\mathsf{Cm}
to
\mathsf{Tn}
)
.

Let
\mathsf{Cm}=(\vec{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\vec{E}},\{\mathcal{T}^{v}\}_{v\in\vec{V}})
be a causal model. We map it to a tensor network
\mathsf{Tn}=\left(\bar{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\bar{E}},\{P_{v}\}_{v\in\bar{V}}\right)
on an undirected graph
\bar{G}=(\bar{V},\bar{E})
, as follows:

1.

the undirected graph
\bar{G}
has the same vertex and edge set as
\vec{G}
, where the edges are deprived of ordering
4
4

4

It might be that
\vec{G}
has edges
(v,w)
and
(w,v)
forming a cycle of two vertices. In this case, add to
\bar{G}
two edges
\{v,w\}
and associate the Hilbert spaces according to those of the causal model.
, i.e., to each ordered pair
(v,w)\in\vec{E}
corresponds a set
\{v,w\}\in\bar{E}
;

2.

the Hilbert space associated to
e\in\bar{E}
is the same associated to the corresponding directed edge in
\vec{G}
in
\mathsf{Cm}
;

3.

to each vertex
v\in\bar{V}
we associate
P_{v}=\mathbb{J}_{\mathrm{Out}(v)|\mathrm{In}(v)}\left(\mathcal{E}^{v}\right)
if
v
is unobserved in the causal graph of
\mathsf{Cm}
, and
P_{v}=\mathbb{J}_{\mathrm{Out}(v)|\mathrm{In}(v)}\left(\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{v}}\mathcal{M}_{v}^{x}\right)
if
v
is observed.

Thus,

\rho_{P}=\bigotimes_{v\in\bar{V}}P_{v}=\mathbb{J}_{\vec{E}_{\textup{out}}|\vec{E}_{\textup{in}}}\left(\sum_{x}\mathcal{C}^{x}\right),

(51)

where
\mathcal{H}_{\vec{E}_{\textup{in}}}\cong\mathcal{H}_{\vec{E}_{\textup{out}}}\cong\mathcal{H}_{\vec{E}}
and
\mathcal{C}^{x}
is the total map of
\mathsf{Cm}
.

We denote the image tensor network of a given causal model as
\varphi(\mathsf{Cm})=\mathsf{Tn}
.

Notice that the mapping gives a well-defined tensor network as for all
v\in\bar{V}
,
P_{v}
is a valid density operator. Indeed, this follows by noticing that the sum over the CP maps of observed vertices is CPTP and by
Lemma

2
.

Remark 2
.

The mapping of
Definition

13
is defined for arbitrary causal models through marginalising over the set of CP maps associated to observed vertices before applying the Choi-Jamiołkowski isomorphism. Equivalently, one can formulate the mapping for causal models with only unobserved vertices as the results involving signalling and causal influence are unaffected by the way we model observed variables. Indeed, according to
Definition

12
observed variables, which are not arising from interventions, are marginalised.

From a mathematical perspective, considering only causal models with unobserved vertices is not restrictive, as observed outcomes and their statistics can be studied explicitly through interventions. However, from a fundamental perspective, one can think of purely unobserved causal models as describing underlying evolutions in a theory that are not being changed through interventions of the agents under consideration.

Acyclic example

Let us consider as an example the causal model of a Bell scenario, on the graph

\vec{G}=\ \vbox{\hbox{\hbox to79.11pt{\vbox to58.83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.44272pt\lower-8.075pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

(52)

Let us label the Hilbert spaces as follows:
\mathcal{H}_{(X,A)}=\mathcal{H}_{X}
,
\mathcal{H}_{(L,A)}=\mathcal{H}_{L_{1}}
,
\mathcal{H}_{(L,B)}=\mathcal{H}_{L_{2}}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{(Y,B)}=\mathcal{H}_{Y}
.
To the exogenous vertices we associate the states

\rho_{X}^{x}=p_{x}^{X}|x\rangle\langle x|_{X}\quad\rho_{Y}^{y}=p_{y}^{Y}|y\rangle\langle y|_{Y}\quad\rho_{L}=|\Phi^{+}\rangle\langle\Phi^{+}|_{L_{1}L_{2}}

(53)

and to the observed vertices
A
and
B
the maps

\mathcal{M}_{a}^{A}(\cdot)=\mathrm{Tr}_{L_{1}X}\left[E_{a}^{A}(\cdot)\right]\quad\textup{and}\quad\mathcal{M}_{b}^{B}(\cdot)=\mathrm{Tr}_{L_{2}Y}\left[E_{b}^{B}(\cdot)\right].

(54)

The image tensor network is defined on the graph

L
A
B
X
Y

(55)

and has tensors:

P_{X}=\sum_{x}p_{x}^{X}|x\rangle\langle x|_{X},\quad P_{Y}=\sum_{y}p_{y}^{Y}|y\rangle\langle y|_{Y},\quad P_{L}=|\Phi^{+}\rangle\langle\Phi^{+}|_{L_{1}L_{2}}

(56)

and

P_{A}=\mathbb{J}_{|XL_{1}}\left(\sum_{a}\mathcal{M}_{a}^{A}\right),\quad P_{B}=\mathbb{J}_{|YL_{2}}\left(\sum_{b}\mathcal{M}_{b}^{B}\right).

(57)

Cyclic example

Let us consider a causal model whose underlying graph is cyclic. We have

(58)

where we labelled the Hilbert spaces as
\mathcal{H}_{(L_{1},L_{2})}=\mathcal{H}_{A}
and
\mathcal{H}_{(L_{2},L_{1})}=\mathcal{H}_{B}
. We associate to the vertices the CPTP maps

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}^{L_{1}}:\mathcal{H}_{B}\mapsto\mathcal{H}_{A}\quad\textup{and}\quad\mathcal{E}^{L_{2}}:\mathcal{H}_{A}\mapsto\mathcal{H}_{B}.\end{split}

(59)

The image tensor network is defined on the graph

L_{1}
L_{2}

(60)

and has tensors

\begin{split}P_{L_{1}}=\mathbb{J}_{A|B}\left(\mathcal{E}^{L_{1}}\right)\quad\textup{and}\quad P_{L_{2}}=\mathbb{J}_{B|A}\left(\mathcal{E}^{L_{2}}\right).\end{split}

(61)

4.2
Mapping tensor networks to causal models

Now we present the mapping in the opposite direction, from tensor networks to causal models. The goal is to construct a mapping which always allows to obtain a causal model from a given tensor network. We achieve this in two steps: first we present a mapping which preserves the graph of the tensor network, up to introducing directionality, but does not provide a causal model for arbitrary directions, then we generalise it showing that, up to introducing further cyclicity, each choice of direction in the tensor networks maps to a valid causal model.

4.2.1
Mapping for a restricted case

Firstly, we define how to construct a causal graph associated to a given tensor network.

Definition 14

(Causal graph of
\mathsf{Tn}
)
.

Let us consider an undirected graph
\bar{G}=(\bar{V},\bar{E})
with vertices labelled by distinct integers
5
5

5

Such labelling can always be arbitrarily chosen.
. Given a binary string
D=\{d_{e}\}_{e\in\bar{E}}
,
d_{e}\in\{0,1\}
, we construct a directed causal graph
\vec{G}_{D}=(\vec{V},\vec{E})
as follows:

1.

the vertex set
\vec{V}
of
\vec{G}_{D}
equals those of
\bar{G}
, i.e.,
\vec{V}=\bar{V}
, and all vertices are unobserved;

2.

for each edge
e=\{v_{i},v_{j}\}\in\bar{E}
with
i<j
, add to
\vec{E}
the ordered pair
(v_{i},v_{j})
if
d_{e}=0
or the ordered pair
(v_{j},v_{i})
if
d_{e}=1
.

In words, the string
D
encodes a choice of direction
d_{e}
for each undirected edge in the graph
\bar{G}
. The directed graph
\vec{G}_{D}
is then obtained implementing such choice. For each causal graph constructed in this way, we can now attempt to define a causal model.

Definition 15

(Mapping
\mathsf{Tn}
to
\mathsf{Cm}
)
.

Let
\mathsf{Tn}=(\bar{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\bar{E}},\{P_{v}\}_{v\in\bar{V}})
be a tensor network, and consider a binary string
D\in\{0,1\}^{|\bar{E}|}
. Let
\vec{G}_{D}
be the directed graph constructed through
Definition

14
. If the density operators of the tensor network satisfy

\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{Out}(v)}[P_{v}]=\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{In}(v)}}{d_{\mathrm{In}(v)}}

(62)

for all
v\in\vec{V}
where
\mathrm{Out}(v)
and
\mathrm{In}(v)
are relative to
\vec{G}_{D}
and where
d_{\mathrm{In}(v)}=\dim(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{In}(v)})
, we map the tensor network to a causal model
\mathsf{Cm}=(\vec{G}_{D},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\vec{E}},\{\mathcal{T}^{v}\}_{v\in\vec{V}})
obtained as follows:

1.

to each directed edge
e=(v,w)\in\vec{E}
associate the Hilbert space associated with the corresponding undirected edge in
\bar{G}
,
\mathcal{H}_{e}
;

2.

to each vertex
v\in\vec{V}
, unobserved by construction, associate the CPTP map

\mathcal{E}^{v}=\mathbb{J}^{-1}_{\mathrm{Out}(v)|\mathrm{In}(v)}\left(P_{v}\right).

(63)

We denote the image causal model of a given tensor network as
\psi_{D}(\mathsf{Tn})
.

Given a choice of orientation of the undirected graph underlying the tensor network, the above definition defines a causal model only if the trace condition in
equation

62
is satisfied. Such condition ensures that the maps associated to vertices of the causal model are CPTP, and as a consequence, it ensures that the tensor network is mapped to a well-defined causal model. According to this construction, a tensor network can be mapped to multiple causal models or none depending on the maps. For instance, consider the tensor network

A
B

(64)

where
P_{A}
and
P_{B}
are two density operators different from the maximally mixed state. Since there is only one edge, two choices of
D
are possible, leading to the directed graphs

D={0}:\quad\vec{G}_{0}=\ \vbox{\hbox{\hbox to74.16pt{\vbox to17.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 37.02776pt\lower-8.67485pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{

{{}}{}{{}}
{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {}{

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{-15.24017pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.22 0 L 0.69 2.67 L 0 0 L 0.69 -2.67 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\ .

(65)

One can define a causal model on
\vec{G}_{0}
and
\vec{G}_{1}
, if respectively

\begin{split}D={0}:\quad\mathrm{Tr}(P_{A})=1\textup{ and }P_{B}=\frac{\mathbbm{1}}{d}\\
D={1}:\quad P_{A}=\frac{\mathbbm{1}}{d}\textup{ and }\mathrm{Tr}(P_{B})=1\end{split}

(66)

since we assumed that
P_{A}
and
P_{B}
are both different from the maximally mixed state, the tensor network cannot be mapped to a causal model.
Notice that if we assume that only one between
P_{A}
and
P_{B}
is equal to the maximally mixed state, we obtain exactly one causal model associated to the tensor network. While if
P_{A}=P_{B}=\frac{\mathbbm{1}}{d}
, both choices lead to a valid causal model.

In the next paragraph, we propose a generalisation of the mapping that allows us to map a given tensor network to a causal model for each choice of
D
.
In
Section

6
, we will study more complex examples of the mapping and their application, specifically in the case of holographic tensor network
[
PYHP15
]
.

4.2.2
Mapping for the general case

In
Definition

15
, we only map a tensor network to causal models which fulfils the partial trace condition in
equation

62
. If such a condition is not satisfied in
\vec{G}_{D}
for any
D\in\{0,1\}^{|\bar{E}|}
, then the mapping of
Definition

15
is not defined for such a tensor network. In this section, we provide a more general mapping which allows us to map a given tensor network to valid causal models for all choices of direction
D
.
The idea is to construct another directed graph
\vec{G}_{D}^{\circlearrowleft}
which introduces self-cycles for all vertices that do not fulfil
equation

62
. Then, by using the results of
[
JSV25
]
we can map the tensor network to a valid causal model on
\vec{G}_{D}^{\circlearrowleft}
.

Lemma 3
.

Let
\mathsf{Tn}=(\bar{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\bar{E}},\{P_{v}\}_{v\in\bar{V}})
be a tensor network,
D\in\{0,1\}^{|\bar{E}|}
a binary string and
\vec{G}_{D}
the associated directed graph constructed according to
Definition

14
, with ingoing and outgoing edges to
v\in\vec{V}_{D}
denotes as
\mathrm{In}(v)
and
\mathrm{Out}(v)
.
For all
v\in\bar{V}
, the density operator
P_{v}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Adj}(v)}\right)\cong\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{In}(v)}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Out}(v)}\right)
, satisfies

\mathbb{J}^{-1}_{\mathrm{Out}(v)|\mathrm{In}(v)}\left(P_{v}\right)=\alpha_{v}\mathrm{cycle}_{A_{v}}(\mathcal{E}^{v}),

(67)

where
\alpha_{v}
is a real strictly positive constant and
\mathcal{E}^{v}
is a CPTP map

\mathcal{E}^{v}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{A_{v}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{In}(v)})\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{A_{v}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Out}(v)}),

(68)

and
\mathcal{H}_{A_{v}}
is an ancillary Hilbert space of dimension
\dim_{A_{v}}=2\dim_{\mathrm{In}(v)}
.

Proof.

The proof uses results from
[
JSV25
]
, see
Appendix

A
.
∎

Definition 16

(General mapping
\mathsf{Tn}
to
\mathsf{Cm}
)
.

Let
\mathsf{Tn}=(\bar{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\bar{E}},\{P_{v}\}_{v\in\bar{V}})
be a tensor network and
D\in\{0,1\}^{|\bar{E}|}
a binary string. Consider the directed graph
\vec{G}_{D}=(\vec{V},\vec{E})
constructed from
\bar{G}
and
D
through
Definition

14
, and let
V^{\neg}\subseteq\vec{V}
be the set of vertices for which
equation

62
is not satisfied, i.e.,
v\in V^{\neg}
if and only if

\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathrm{Out}(v)}[P_{v}]\neq\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{In}(v)}}{d_{\mathrm{In}(v)}}.

(69)

Then, define the directed graph
\vec{G}_{D}^{\circlearrowleft}=(\vec{V}^{\circlearrowleft},\vec{E}^{\circlearrowleft})
from
\vec{G}_{D}
as follows:
\vec{V}^{\circlearrowleft}=\vec{V}
and
\vec{E}^{\circlearrowleft}=\vec{E}\cup\{(v,v)\}_{v\in V^{\neg}}
, i.e., the graphs are the same up to adding self-loops for all vertices in
V^{\neg}
. We map the tensor network
\mathsf{Tn}
to a causal model on the directed graph
\vec{G}^{\circlearrowleft}_{D}
as follows:

1.

to each directed edge
e=(v,w)\in\vec{E}\subseteq\vec{E}^{\circlearrowleft}
associate in
\vec{G}_{D}^{\circlearrowleft}
the Hilbert space associated with the corresponding undirected edge in
\bar{G}
,
\mathcal{H}_{e}
;

2.

to each vertex
v\in\vec{V}\backslash V^{\neg}
associate the CPTP map
6
6

6

Notice that this is CPTP since
equation

62
is satisfied by construction

\mathcal{E}^{v}=\mathbb{J}^{-1}_{\mathrm{Out}(v)|\mathrm{In}(v)}\left(P_{v}\right);

(70)

3.

to each directed edge
e=(v,v)\in\vec{E}^{\circlearrowleft}\backslash\vec{E}
, associate the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{A_{v}}
of the ancilla system
A_{v}
as defined in
Lemma

3
;

4.

to each vertex
v\in\vec{V}^{\neg}
, associate the CPTP map

\mathcal{E}^{v}:\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{A_{v}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{In}(v)}\right)\mapsto\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{A_{v}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Out}(v)}\right)

(71)

satisfying

\mathrm{cycle}_{A_{v}}(\mathcal{E}^{v})=\frac{1}{\alpha_{v}}\mathbb{J}^{-1}_{\mathrm{Out}(v)|\mathrm{In}(v)}\left(P_{v}\right),

(72)

for some positive non-zero constant
\alpha_{v}
. Such map and the constant
\alpha_{v}
are explicitly constructed in the proof of
Lemma

3
(see
Appendix

A
).

We denote the image causal model of a given tensor network as
\psi^{\textup{gen}}_{D}(\mathsf{Tn})
.

Notice that the generalised mapping trivially reduces to the one of
Definition

15
if the partial trace condition in
equation

62
is satisfied for all
v\in\bar{V}
. Indeed, in this case the set
V^{\neg}
is empty, and the associations corresponding to the first two items above are equal to those of
Definition

15
.

Remark 3
.

The generalised mapping presented in
Definition

16
explicitly allows to construct the relevant CPTP map, up to introducing an ancillary system having twice the dimension of the input space. Specifically, the ancilla contains a system isomorphic to the input space tensored with a qubit space. A different result of
[
JSV25
]
proves the existence of a CPTP map which also satisfies
equation

67
with ancilla space isomorphic to the input space only. This, however, is an existence argument, and does not fully specify the map
\mathcal{E}^{v}
.
Thus, one could modify the above definition of the mapping and obtain an existence statement instead of a constructing one, with ancillas of half the size.
In either case, the result is a causal model on
\vec{G}_{D}^{\circlearrowleft}
where
equation

67
holds for every vertex
v\in\bar{V}
, but the exact maps and dimension of the Hilbert space associated to the additional self-cycle edges
(v,v)
would differ.

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{-15.24017pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.22 0 L 0.69 2.67 L 0 0 L 0.69 -2.67 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}
