{{}{}}{}{{}}
{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}{
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -46.49 -9.49 L -50.54 -14.89 C -57.42 -24.06 -68.57 -24.06 -75.45 -14.89 L -74.16 -16.6 C -81.04 -7.43 -81.04 7.43 -74.16 16.6 L -75.45 14.89 C -68.57 24.06 -57.42 24.06 -50.54 14.89 L -50.55 14.9}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.6}{-0.8}{0.8}{0.6}{-36.38039pt}{10.5701pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.22 0 L 0.69 2.67 L 0 0 L 0.69 -2.67 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\ .

(75)

To give an explicit example, consider a tensor network on
\bar{G}
with a qubit Hilbert space associated to the unique edge, and let us denote the system as
S
, i.e.,
\mathcal{H}_{e}\equiv\mathcal{H}_{S}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and take
P_{A}=|+\rangle\langle+|
and
P_{B}=|0\rangle\langle 0|
.
Then, for
D=0
the image causal model on
\vec{G}^{\circlearrowleft}_{D=0}
is given by associating a qubit space to the ancilla system, which we denote as
H
, and maps
\rho_{A}=P^{A}
and
\mathcal{E}^{B}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{H})\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{H})
where
\mathcal{E}^{B}=\textup{CNOT}_{S\to H}
with control on the system
S
.
Indeed, we have

\mathbb{J}^{-1}_{|S}(|0\rangle\langle 0|)(\rho_{S})=d_{H}\sum_{i,j}\langle 0|i\rangle\langle j|\rho_{S}|i\rangle\langle j|0\rangle=d_{H}\langle 0|\rho_{S}|0\rangle|0\rangle\langle 0|_{S}

(76)

and on the other hand

\mathrm{cycle}_{H}(\text{CNOT}_{S\to H})(\rho_{S})=\sum_{i,j}\langle i|(\text{CNOT}_{S\to H}(\rho_{S}\otimes|i\rangle\langle j|)|j\rangle=\langle 0|\rho_{S}|0\rangle|0\rangle\langle 0|_{S}.

(77)

For
D=1
, the result is analogous up to applying a Hadamard gate before the CNOT.

The intuition behind this construction is that the instances where the ancilla system is flipped lead to a null post-selection probability in the self-cycle. Thus, this effectively reduces the CPTP map on the system and ancilla to a CP map on the system which represents the action in the case where the ancilla is not flipped (in the CNOT, this corresponds to projecting the system, which is the control, on
|0\rangle
).

4.3
Properties of the mappings

In this section, we provide properties of the mappings that we have just constructed (
Definitions

13
,
15
and
16
). The proofs of the results in this section can be found in
Appendix

A
.

Composition of mappings

A first relevant property involves the composition of the two mappings, which is the content of the following lemma.

Lemma 4
.

Let
\mathsf{Cm}=(\vec{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\vec{E}},\{\mathcal{T}^{v}\}_{v\in\vec{V}})
be a causal model such that
V_{\textup{o}}=\emptyset
and assume that the vertices of
\vec{G}
are labelled by distinct integers. Define the binary string
D_{\vec{G}}=\{d_{e}\}_{e\in\vec{E}}
where for
e=(v_{i},v_{j})\in\vec{E}
,
d_{e}=0
if
j\geq i
and
d_{e}=1
if
j<i
, i.e., the sting
D_{\vec{G}}
simply records the direction of each edge in the directed graph
\vec{G}
. Then, it holds:

\psi_{D_{\vec{G}}}\circ\varphi(\mathsf{Cm})=\mathsf{Cm},

(78)

where
\varphi
is as in
Definition

13
and
\psi
as in
Definition

15
.
Similarly, consider a tensor network
\mathsf{Tn}=\left(\bar{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\bar{E}},\{P_{v}\}_{v\in\bar{V}}\right)
and any
D\in\{0,1\}^{|\bar{E}|}
such that the mapping
\psi_{D}
is well defined, then it holds:

\varphi\circ\psi_{D}(\mathsf{Tn})=\mathsf{Tn}.

(79)

Proof.

See
Appendix

A
.
∎

Notice that, when keeping track of the direction of the initial directed graph, the mappings define an isomorphism. This has to be expected as a given tensor network is uniquely mapped into a causal model only for a fixed and allowed
D
. This property is analogous to the Choi-Jamiołkowsi isomorphism, which is indeed an isomorphism only for a fixed choice of input and output in the inverse Choi.
Equation

78
is valid only for causal models with no observed vertices. This is not restrictive for our purpose, as already argued in
Remark

2
. Indeed, as will see in the following section, for studying signalling properties of the mappings, the relevant quantities always involve marginalization over the observed outcomes of the model.

In the case of the generalised mapping,
equation

78
is trivially satisfied by replacing
\psi_{D_{\vec{G}}}
with
\psi^{\textup{gen}}_{D_{\vec{G}}}
since all partial trace conditions are satisfied by construction, thus the two mappings are the same for such choice of direction
D_{\vec{G}}
. However,
equation

79
is not exactly satisfied for the generalised mapping because of the addition of self-loops in the causal model. The following holds instead:

Lemma 5
.

Consider a tensor network
\mathsf{Tn}=\left(\bar{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\bar{E}},\{P_{v}\}_{v\in\bar{V}}\right)
and
D\in\{0,1\}^{|\bar{E}|}
, then it holds:

\varphi\circ\psi^{\textup{gen}}_{D}(\mathsf{Tn})=\left(\bar{G}^{\circlearrowleft},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}^{\circlearrowleft}\}_{e\in\bar{E}^{\circlearrowleft}},\{P_{v}^{\circlearrowleft}\}_{v\in\bar{V}}\right),

(80)

where

1.

the undirected graph
\bar{G}^{\circlearrowleft}
has the same vertex and edge set as
\vec{G}^{\circlearrowleft}
, the directed graph of the causal model
\psi^{\textup{gen}}_{D}(\mathsf{Tn})
according to
Definition

16
, where the edges are deprived of ordering, i.e., to each ordered pair
(v,w)\in\vec{E}^{\circlearrowleft}
corresponds a set
\{v,w\}\in\bar{E}^{\circlearrowleft}
;

2.

\mathcal{H}_{e}^{\circlearrowleft}=\mathcal{H}_{e}
for all
e\in\bar{E}\subseteq\bar{E}^{\circlearrowleft}
;

3.

for all
v\in\bar{V}
it holds

\langle\Omega^{+}|_{\bar{E}(v)}P_{v}^{\circlearrowleft}|\Omega^{+}\rangle_{\bar{E}(v)}=\beta_{v}P_{v}

(81)

where
\beta_{v}>0
is a real constant and
\bar{E}(v)
is the set of edges adjacent to
v
in
\bar{G}^{\circlearrowleft}
but not in
\bar{G}
, i.e.,
\bar{E}(v)=\mathrm{Adj}(v)_{\bar{G}^{\circlearrowleft}}\setminus\mathrm{Adj}(v)_{\bar{G}}
.

Proof.

See
Appendix

A
.
∎

There is nevertheless a natural sense in which the original tensor network is recovered. Starting from the right-hand side of
equation

80
, one may perform only a subset of the tensor network contractions, i.e., those corresponding to the added self-cycle edges, while leaving all other edges uncontracted.
This suggests introducing the notion of a reduced tensor network, analogous to the concept of reduced process matrices or, more generally, to partial composition of cyclic quantum networks as discussed in
[
VR24a
]
. By a reduced tensor network, we mean a network obtained by contracting only a specified subset of edges of a larger network, thereby producing a new tensor network on the corresponding subgraph. In this sense, the original tensor network is recovered as the reduced tensor network on the subgraph
\bar{G}
of
\bar{G}^{\circlearrowleft}
, obtained by contracting precisely the self-cycle edges that were introduced.

Tensor contraction and self-cycle

The following lemma proves that two quantities which are relevant respectively for tensor networks and causal models, namely the tensor network contraction and the self-cycle, are linked through the mappings.
This property is crucial in connecting causal influence to known notions of causality.

Lemma 6
.

Let us consider a causal model on
\vec{G}
and a tensor network on
\bar{G}
,
\mathsf{Cm}
and
\mathsf{Tn}
, such that
\varphi(\mathsf{Cm})=\mathsf{Tn}
or
\psi^{\textup{gen}}_{D}(\mathsf{Tn})=\mathsf{Cm}
for some
D\in\{0,1\}^{|\bar{E}|}
. The marginalised total map of
\mathsf{Cm}
,
\sum_{x}\mathcal{C}^{x}=\mathcal{C}
, and the link and total state of
\mathsf{Tn}
,
|L\rangle
,
\rho_{P}
, satisfy:

\beta\;\mathrm{cycle}\left(\mathcal{C}\right)=\langle L|\rho_{P}|L\rangle,

(82)

for some real constant
\beta>0
determined solely by the constants
\alpha_{v}
of
Definition

16
and the dimensions of
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{E}}
.

Proof.

See
Appendix

A
.
∎

Then, the following corollary follows straightforwardly.

Corollary 7
.

Given a tensor network
\mathsf{Tn}
on
\bar{G}
and two choices of directions
D_{1},D_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{|E|}
, then the marginalised total maps of
\psi^{\textup{gen}}_{D_{1}}(\mathsf{Tn})
and
\psi^{\textup{gen}}_{D_{2}}(\mathsf{Tn})
satisfy

\beta\mathrm{cycle}\left(\mathcal{C}_{1}\right)=\mathrm{cycle}\left(\mathcal{C}_{2}\right),

(83)

for some real constant
\beta>0
determined by the constants
\alpha_{v}
of
Definition

16
and the dimensions of
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{E}}
.

This property has an interesting feature: a given causal model
\mathsf{Cm}_{1}
can be first mapped into a tensor network through
\varphi(\mathsf{Cm}_{1})=\mathsf{Tn}
, then the image tensor network can be mapped to a different causal model from the original one,
\mathsf{Cm}_{2}
. Because of
Corollary

7
, it holds:

\mathrm{cycle}\left(\mathcal{C}_{1}\right)=\beta\mathrm{cycle}\left(\mathcal{C}_{2}\right),

(84)

where
\mathcal{C}_{i}
is the marginalised total map of the causal model
\mathsf{Cm}_{i}
and
\beta=1
if the mapping from
\mathsf{Tn}
to
\mathsf{Cm}_{2}
is associated with a valid choice of directions according to
Definition

15
,
D
, while generally
\beta\in\mathbb{C}
.
Thus, one can use the mapping to construct a family of causal models that are closely related to each other. In the following, we will explore this feature even further, arguing that this class of causal models, arising from the same tensor network can be understood as space-time rotations of each other.

5
Signalling in causal models vs causal influence in tensor networks

In this section, we show a connection between causal influence in tensor networks and signalling in causal models through the mappings defined in
Section

4
.

First, we analyse the definition of causal influence in tensor networks and present a slight modification of it, providing it with a more operational interpretation, which will allow us to definitively conclude that it does capture causation in an operational sense, as opposed to generic correlations.

5.1
Operational analysis of causal influence proposed in
[
CHQY19
]

The mappings of
Section

4
highlight connections between the tensor network contraction and the self-cycle operation.
These two quantities are relevant respectively in defining causal influence in a tensor network (see
Definition

4
) and probabilities in a casual model (see
Definition

9
). Thus, we can use the connection between probabilities, which are operational quantities, and self-cycle to provide causal influence with an operational motivation (see
Section

2.3
).

First, we recall the definition of causal influence provided in
[
CHQY19
]
and analyse its operational interpretation in connection with probability theory.

See
[use
cref
not
ref
]

The arguments leading to the above definition have been discussed in
Section

2.2
. Now we know that the correlation functions appearing in
Definition

4
are connected to the self-cycle operation, which is related to probabilities in cyclic causal models
Lemma

6
. We can thus rephrase the arguments leading to
Definition

4
in terms of probabilities. For now, we will make these arguments in terms of “virtual” measurements, whose physicality is unclear. Thus, the following are to be understood as intuitive arguments clarifying
Definition

4
, these will be made precise though the connection between causal influence and signalling in causal models (
Appendices

C
and
5
):

•

The tensor network contraction:
The tensor network contraction of
Definition

3
can be written as
\langle L|\rho_{P}|L\rangle=\mathrm{Tr}\left(|L\rangle\langle L|\rho_{P}\right)\in\mathbb{C}
. If we define a binary measurement
\{|L\rangle\langle L|,\mathbbm{1}-|L\rangle\langle L|\}
, associated with outcomes
\{L,\neg L\}
, we can understand the tensor network contraction as a probability of observing outcome corresponding to the POVM element
|L\rangle\langle L|
on the state
\rho_{P}
, and denote it with
P(L)_{\rho_{P}}
.
This “virtual” measurement on the
\rho_{P}
will then be linked to observable probabilities in acyclic casual models via our mapping from tensor networks to causal models (see
Appendices

C
and
5
).

•

The denominator:
The role of the denominator is intuitively explained in
[
CHQY19
]
by saying that it is needed in order to isolate the influence on
\mathcal{B}
of modifications in
\mathcal{A}
from the influence that these have on the whole network contraction. With the interpretation given above, we can understand the denominator as a probability of observing outcome corresponding to the POVM element
|L\rangle\langle L|
on the state
U_{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{P}U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}
, i.e.,
P(L)_{U_{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{P}U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}}
.

We remark that the denominator may be zero, in case
P(L)_{U_{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{P}U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}}=0
.

•

The operation inserted in
\mathcal{B}
:
The operator
O_{\mathcal{B}}
inserted in
\mathcal{B}
is required to be Hermitian, so that no predefined direction is induced, and such that
O^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{B}}O_{\mathcal{B}}\leq\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{B}}
. Consider now a quantum instrument, corresponding to a measurement of a quantum system including the quantum evolution of the measured state. This is described by a linear, completely positive and trace preserving map, which can be decomposed in the Kraus form as

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}X|\mathcal{B}}(\rho_{\mathcal{B}})=\sum_{x}|x\rangle\langle x|_{X}\otimes O^{x}_{\mathcal{B}}\rho_{\mathcal{B}}{O^{x}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}

(85)

for some set of positive linear operators,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}}=\{O^{x}_{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}})\}_{x}
, which we call Kraus operators and satisfy
\sum_{x}{O^{x}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}O^{x}_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{B}}
. The un-normalised state of the system
\mathcal{B}
after the instrument is applied is

\mathcal{M}^{X=x}_{\mathcal{B}|\mathcal{B}}(\rho_{\mathcal{B}})=O^{x}_{\mathcal{B}}\rho_{\mathcal{B}}{O^{x}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger},

(86)

the above contains the probability associated to observing the outcome
X=x
, which is given by
\mathrm{Tr}(O^{x}_{\mathcal{B}}\rho_{\mathcal{B}}{O^{x}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger})
.
Notice that each
O^{x}_{\mathcal{B}}
satisfies
{O^{x}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger}O^{x}_{\mathcal{B}}\leq\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{B}}
.

Thus, combining this with the previous points, we can understand the operation inserted in
\mathcal{B}
in
Definition

4
as being a Kraus operator corresponding to a specific outcome of a quantum instrument. The correlation function is then related to the probability of observing such outcome, i.e.,
P(L,x|\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})_{U_{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{P}U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}}
.
With this analogy in mind, we can think of the action of modifying the network as applying a quantum instrument, associated with an observable outcome, in the region
\mathcal{B}
and observing whether the probability of such outcome is affected by local modifications in
\mathcal{A}
.

Thus, by putting together the above points we find that we can understand the quantity
M_{\textup{CHQY}}
as

M_{\textup{CHQY}}(U_{\mathcal{A}}:O_{\mathcal{B}})=\frac{\langle L|(U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes O_{\mathcal{B}})\rho_{P}(U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\dagger}\otimes O_{\mathcal{B}}^{\dagger})|L\rangle}{\langle L|(U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{B}})\rho_{P}(U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{B}})|L\rangle}=\frac{P(L,\bar{x}|\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})_{U_{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{P}U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}}}{P(L)_{U_{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{P}U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}}},

(87)

for an arbitrary set
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}}=\{O^{x}_{\mathcal{B}}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
such that
O_{\mathcal{B}}=O_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bar{x}}\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}}
for some
\bar{x}\in\mathcal{X}
provided in
Definition

4
.
This definition should have the goal of isolating the effect of
U_{\mathcal{A}}
on the outcome of the measurement in
\mathcal{B}
from its effect on the outcome of the tensor network contraction,
L
. However, this effect, which would be naturally portrayed through conditional probabilities, cannot be justified operationally. Indeed, in general,
\sum_{x}P(L,x|\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})_{U_{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{P}U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}}\neq P(L)_{U_{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{P}U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}}
, thus the quantity
M_{\textup{CHQY}}
is not a probability distribution and
Definition

4
does not have a straightforward operational interpretation in terms of probabilities.

In addition,
Definition

4
does not account for the possibility that the denominator is zero, in which case
M_{\textup{CHQY}}(U_{\mathcal{A}}:O_{\mathcal{B}})
is ill-defined.

5.2
Defining operational causal influence in tensor networks

Let us now provide a different definition of causal influence in a tensor network and show its operational meaning.

Definition 17

(Operational causal influence in a tensor network)
.

Consider a tensor network
\mathsf{Tn}=\left(\bar{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\bar{E}},\{P_{v}\}_{v\in\bar{V}}\right)
and an ordered pair of disjoint subsets of edges, i.e.,
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
such that
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\subseteq\bar{E}
and
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}=\emptyset
, which we call
regions
. Let
U_{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})
be a unitary and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}}=\{O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}})\}_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}
a quantum instrument with Hermitian Kraus operators
O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}}
. We define the correlation function

M(U_{\mathcal{A}}:O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}}|\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})=\frac{\langle L|(U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}})\rho_{P}(U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\dagger}\otimes{O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger})|L\rangle}{\sum_{y}\langle L|(U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}})\rho_{P}(U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\dagger}\otimes{O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger})|L\rangle},

(88)

if
\sum_{y}\langle L|(U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}})\rho_{P}(U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\dagger}\otimes{O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger})|L\rangle\neq 0
. Otherwise, we say that the pair
U_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}}
are inconsistent relative to the tensor network.
The operational quantum causal influence from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
is zero if and only if

M(U_{\mathcal{A}}:O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}}|\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})=M(U^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}}:O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}}|\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})

(89)

for all unitaries
U_{\mathcal{A}}

U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
and quantum instruments
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}}=\{O_{\mathcal{B}}^{y}\}_{y}
such that
(U_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})
and
(U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})
are both consistent pairs.

Note that above definition of causal influence depends not just on the particular instrument element
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}}^{y}
but on the the entire instrument
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}}=\{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}}^{y}\}_{y}
, which we have made explicit by introducing a conditioning on
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}}
in the notation for the quantity
M
.
Furthermore, the above definition also takes care of situations where the denominator is zero, thus providing a well-defined notion of the quantity
M
and causal influence. In addition, we can interpret
M
as

\begin{split}M(U_{\mathcal{A}}:O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}}|\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})&=\frac{\langle L|(U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}})\rho_{P}(U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\dagger}\otimes{O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger})|L\rangle}{\sum_{y}\langle L|(U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}})\rho_{P}(U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\dagger}\otimes{O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger})|L\rangle}\\
&=\frac{P(L,y|\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})_{U_{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{P}U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}}}{\sum_{y}P(L,y|\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})_{U_{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{P}U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}}}=P(y|L,\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})_{U_{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{P}U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}},\end{split}

(90)

by following the same interpretation of correlation functions as probabilities.
Conditional probabilities are precisely the object that allows us to isolate the contribution to the observable in
\mathcal{B}
from the contribution to the whole tensor network contraction. Indeed, it evaluates the distribution in
\mathcal{B}
for fixed
L
. We refer to
Definition

17
as “operational causal influence”, however, when clear from context we may simply call this measure “causal influence”.

Going back to the open questions presented in
Section

2.3
, we can now lift the doubt on whether one can define a notion of causal influence in tensor networks which genuinely portrays causation and not solely correlations. Indeed,
Definition

17
provides an asymmetric correlation function where the observed distribution associated to the affected region
\mathcal{B}
is evaluated for different choices of operations in
\mathcal{A}
. This is analogous to definitions of signalling in causal models, as we will further investigate in the next section.

Remark 4
.

Generally, the two definitions of causal influence differ from each other. Indeed, while one only depends on one Kraus operator of a quantum instrument, the other depends on the whole set of operators. Thus, the two differ when different completions of the Kraus operator affect the network contraction in the denominator. The setups where the definition coincide can be studied using linearity results valid for causal models
[
FGV25b
]
and the mappings of the previous section. For instance, we expect the two notions of causal influence to coincide for tensor networks whose image causal model through the mappings of
Section

4
is a process matrix
[
BLO21
]
. Indeed, these are known to be a linear subset of generally cyclic causal models, which implies, thanks to results of
[
FGV25b
]
, that the denominators appearing in the two definitions of causal influence are constant and equal.

5.3
Bridging signalling and operational causal influence

In this section, we show that the mappings of
Section

4
link operational causal influence in a tensor network (
Definition

17
) to signalling with unitaries in causal model as in
Definition

12
.
Firstly, let us define a time-reversible intervention on a given causal model.

Definition 18

(Time-reversible intervention)
.

Let
\mathsf{Cm}=(\vec{G},\{\mathcal{H}_{e}\}_{e\in\vec{E}},\{\mathcal{T}^{v}\}_{v\in\vec{V}})
be a causal model and consider an ordered pair of labs
(\mathbbm{A},\mathbbm{B})
, i.e., disjoint subsets of edges such that
\mathbbm{A},\mathbbm{B}\subseteq\vec{E}
and
\mathbbm{A}\cap\mathbbm{B}=\emptyset
.
We call
time-reversible intervention
an intervention defined by:

•

the set of all unitary channels on
\mathbbm{A}
labelled by a continuous variable
a\in\mathcal{X}_{A}
associated to the lab
\mathbbm{A}
, i.e.,

\{\mathcal{U}_{\mathbbm{A}}^{a}:\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbbm{A}}\right)\mapsto\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbbm{A}}\right)\}_{a\in\mathcal{X}_{A}},

(91)

such that for all
\rho\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbbm{A}}\right)
and
a\in\mathcal{X}_{A}

\mathcal{U}_{\mathbbm{A}}^{a}(\rho)=U^{a}\rho{U^{a}}^{\dagger}

(92)

where
U^{a}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbbm{A}})
is unitary;

•

the set of all Hermitian quantum instruments with discrete outcome
y\in\mathcal{X}_{Y}
, labelled by a continuous variable
b\in\mathcal{X}_{B}
, associated to the lab
\mathbbm{B}
, i.e.,

\left\{\left\{\mathcal{M}_{\mathbbm{B}}^{y|b}:\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbbm{B}}\right)\mapsto\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbbm{B}}\right)\right\}_{y\in\mathcal{X}_{Y}}\right\}_{b\in\mathcal{X}_{B}}

(93)

such that for all
\rho\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbbm{B}})
,
b\in\mathcal{X}_{B}
and
y\in\mathcal{X}_{Y}

\mathcal{M}^{y|b}_{\mathbbm{B}}(\rho_{\mathbbm{B}})=O^{y|b}_{\mathbbm{B}}\rho_{\mathbbm{B}}{O^{y|b}_{\mathbbm{B}}}^{\dagger},

(94)

where
O^{y|b}_{\mathbbm{B}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbbm{B}})
are positive, Hermitian and
\sum_{y}{O^{y|b}_{\mathbbm{B}}}^{\dagger}O^{y|b}_{\mathbbm{B}}=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbbm{B}}
.

The interventions that we study are time-reversible since the set of unitaries is closed under reversing inputs and outputs and Hermitian operators are invariant under such transformation. The definition is motivated by causal influence in tensor networks, where these operations are considered as they do not introduce an artificial time direction. While isometries are more general time-reversible operations, these are necessarily unitaries because, by construction of interventions, we only consider operations with isomorphic input and output spaces.

Let us now show the connection between signalling and operational causal influence.

Theorem 8

(Connecting signalling and operational causal influence)
.

Let us consider a causal model on
\vec{G}
and a tensor network on
\bar{G}
,
\mathsf{Cm}
and
\mathsf{Tn}
, such that
\varphi(\mathsf{Cm})=\mathsf{Tn}
or
\psi^{\textup{gen}}_{D}(\mathsf{Tn})=\mathsf{Cm}
for some
D\in\{0,1\}^{|\bar{E}|}
. Let
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
be an ordered pair of regions in
\bar{G}
, i.e., disjoint subsets of
\vec{E}
, and
(\mathbbm{A},\mathbbm{B})
be the corresponding ordered pair of labs (cf.
Definition

10
) in the directed graph of
\mathsf{Cm}
. Then,
\mathbbm{A}
signals to
\mathbbm{B}
in
\mathsf{Cm}
through a time-reversible intervention if and only if the operational causal influence from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
in
\mathsf{Tn}
is non-zero.

Proof.

See
Appendix

A
.
∎

With the above theorem, we have proven the central result of the mapping, which allows to interchangeably work between operational causal influence and signalling. Because of this connection, we will often use the terms of labs and regions interchangeably when studying causal influence through an image causal model or signalling through the image tensor network.

Remark 5
.

Thanks to the above connection between signalling in causal models and operational causal influence in tensor networks, we can translate the analysis of
Section

5.1
(relating to the “virtual” measurement) in terms of measurements in acyclic casual models. Specifically, we recall
equation

90
:

\begin{split}M(U_{\mathcal{A}}:O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}}|\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})&=\frac{\langle L|(U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}})\rho_{P}(U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\dagger}\otimes{O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger})|L\rangle}{\sum_{y}\langle L|(U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}})\rho_{P}(U_{\mathcal{A}}^{\dagger}\otimes{O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}}}^{\dagger})|L\rangle}\\
&=\frac{P(L,y|\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})_{U_{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{P}U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}}}{\sum_{y}P(L,y|\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})_{U_{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{P}U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}}}=P(y|L,\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})_{U_{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{P}U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}},\end{split}

(95)

which hints at connections between operational causal influence and conditional probabilities linked to a “virtual measurement” yielding outcome
L
. In
Appendix

C
, we make this connection precise using our mapping from tensor networks to cyclic causal models, and the results of
[
FGV25b
]
which showed that for any possibly cyclic causal model, one can define a family of acyclic causal models with post-selection which share the distribution of the original model up to conditioning on the post-selections being successful. Since
M(U_{\mathcal{A}}:O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}}|\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})
equals the observed probability on the outcome
y
of the cyclic causal model
\mathsf{Cm}=\psi^{\textup{gen}}_{D}(\mathsf{Tn})
(see proof of
Theorem

8
in
Appendix

A
for details), this quantity can be further identified with probabilities conditioned on successful post-selection in the family of acyclic casual models associated to
\mathsf{Cm}
.
Hence, we have:

\begin{split}P(y|L,\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})_{U_{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{P}U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}}&=M(U_{\mathcal{A}}:O^{y}_{\mathcal{B}}|\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})\\
&=\mathrm{Pr}(y)_{I}={\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}\left(y\middle|\{t_{i}=\text{✓}\}_{T_{i}\in V_{\textup{post}}}\right)_{{\vec{G}_{\textsc{tp}}}},\end{split}

(96)

where we denoted with
{\vec{G}_{\textsc{tp}}}
the causal graph underlying one of such acyclic causal models and with
T_{i}\in V_{\textup{post}}
the set of vertices on which we post-select, and with
t_{i}=\checkmark
the instance corresponding to successful post-selection. This allows us to better understand the role of the conditioning on this virtual measurement.

Since such property holds for a whole family of acyclic causal models with post-selection, the quantity
M
has a robust operational interpretation in terms of conditional probabilities across various related acyclic causal models. For more details, we refer to
Appendix

C
.

Remark 6
.

In the causal modelling literature, one often refers to the connectivity of the graph and causal model when talking about causal influence. The connectivity does not always coincide with signalling relations, because of
fine-tuning
, for instance in the case of causal mechanisms which “ignore” some inputs. In this work, whenever we mention “causal influence”, it is in relation to tensor networks and we have shown that this notion does not correspond to what is meant in literature for “causal influence” in a causal model, rather, as we have shown in this chapter, it is related to the notion of signalling in a causal model.

5.4
An application: discrete rotations of causal models

The results of the above section have an interesting consequence: a single tensor network can generate multiple, genuinely distinct causal models by choosing different direction assignments. This phenomenon can be viewed as a “discrete space-time rotation” between causal models. For instance, one can consider a unitary associated to a vertex of a causal model
\mathsf{Cm}_{1}

(97)

Although a priori no background space-time is assumed, the input-output directions of the causal model allow us to infer one. Here, the subsystems
A
and
B
can be seen as “space-like” separated, and similarly for
C
and
D
, while the inferred direction of time flows from
A
and
B
to
C
and
D
. Hence, we obtain the space-time directions drawn above.

However, one can first map
\mathsf{Cm}_{1}
to a tensor network
\mathsf{Tn}
, then map the latter to a different causal model
\mathsf{Cm}_{2}
where, for instance, the edges labelled with
B
and
C
have the opposite direction than in
\mathsf{Cm}_{1}
, i.e., the map associated to
\mathsf{Cm}_{2}
in the new model is

\sum_{ijkl}U_{ij}^{kl}\;|l\rangle_{D}\langle k|_{C}|j\rangle_{B}\langle i|_{A}=\ \vbox{\hbox{\hbox to45.69pt{\vbox to78.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 22.71483pt\lower-39.25072pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\ ,

(98)

where the self-loop in the second diagram might be necessary in case the new choice of directions requires the use of the generalised mapping (see
Definition

16
). Following the same arguments used to infer a direction of space-time in
\mathsf{Cm}_{1}
, here we find that these are “rotated” in
\mathsf{Cm}_{2}
. A special case of space-time “rotations” of maps which has already been studied are so-called
dual
unitaries
[
PBCP20
]
. These are unitaries
U_{CD|AB}=\sum_{ijkl}U_{ij}^{kl}\;|l\rangle_{D}|k\rangle_{C}\langle j|_{B}\langle i|_{A}
such that also their “rotated” version
\tilde{U}_{BD|AC}=\sum_{ijkl}U_{ij}^{kl}\;|l\rangle_{D}\langle k|_{C}|j\rangle_{B}\langle i|_{A}
is unitary. Here, we extend the concept of “rotations” of dual unitaries to “rotations” of arbitrary CPTP maps, potentially at the cost of adding self-cycles.

Thus, from considering a set of causal models which are “rotations” of each other, one can infer an emerging direction of space and time for each model. Further principles, such as acyclicity, or preservation of relativistic causality (which excludes retro-causality), can be evoked to determine a preferred emerging direction of space-time. For instance, in the example above where
\mathsf{Cm}_{1}
and
\mathsf{Cm}_{2}
both arise from the same tensor network, in case the self loop is present in
\mathsf{Cm}_{2}
one would favour the space-time emergent from
\mathsf{Cm}_{1}
, as this preserves relativistic causality.

The following corollary, which follows immediately from
Theorem

8
, further establishes a connection, at the level of signalling between causal models which arise from the same tensor network, which can then be understood as an equivalence class of “rotations” of a causal model.

Corollary 9
.

Consider a tensor network
\mathsf{Tn}
on a undirected graph
\bar{G}
, and let
\mathsf{Cm}_{D}=\psi^{\textup{gen}}_{D}(\mathsf{Tn})
for
D\in\{0,1\}^{|E|}
. Let
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
be an ordered pair of regions in
\bar{G}
, and
(\mathbbm{A}_{D},\mathbbm{B}_{D})
be the corresponding ordered pair of labs in the directed graph of
\mathsf{Cm}_{D}
. Then,
\mathsf{Cm}_{D}
present the same time-reversible signalling relations for all
D
, i.e.,
\mathbbm{A}_{D}
time-reversibly signals to
\mathbbm{B}_{D}
in
\mathsf{Cm}_{D}
if and only if
\mathbbm{A}_{D^{\prime}}
time-reversibly signals to
\mathbbm{B}_{D^{\prime}}
in
\mathsf{Cm}_{D^{\prime}}
.

Therefore, one can define equivalence classes of causal models — those arising from the same tensor network — with identical operational constraints. Indeed, although these causal models have different causal graphs, they nevertheless share exactly the same signalling and non-signalling relations. Notably, the observed non-signalling relations in each model may arise from different sources, for instance from
d
- or
p
-separation
[
Pea09
,
FGV25b
]
(see
Appendix

D
) or from fine-tuning, depending on the underlying causal structure. This might connect fine-tuned relations to sound graph separation properties like
d
- or
p
-separation in a different model of the equivalence class.

Remark 7
.

The statements about signalling relations are restricted to the set of time-reversible interventions (
Definition

18
). Since these only involve unitaries and Hermitian operators, they remain well-defined independently of the direction of the edge they are defined to act on. Signalling with arbitrary interventions can be defined for a fixed causal model, where such interventions are valid quantum operations, to a tensor network, but it cannot be transferred to a different causal model because inverting the direction of the edges we intervene upon might lead to a non-CPTP map.

However, one can transfer signalling relations in a causal model, arising from arbitrary maps as interventions on the labs
\mathbbm{A}
and
\mathbbm{B}
, to the same signalling relations in any other model of the equivalence class which has the same directions for the edges
\mathbbm{A}
and
\mathbbm{B}
. Explicitly, given
\mathsf{Cm}_{D}
and
\mathsf{Cm}_{D^{\prime}}
, obtained through
\psi^{\textup{gen}}_{D}
and
\psi^{\textup{gen}}_{D^{\prime}}
from the same tensor network
\mathsf{Tn}
, signalling from
\mathbbm{A}_{D}
to
\mathbbm{B}_{D}
with arbitrary interventions implies the same signalling from
\mathbbm{A}_{D^{\prime}}
to
\mathbbm{B}_{D^{\prime}}
if
\mathbbm{A}_{D}=\mathbbm{A}_{D^{\prime}}
and
\mathbbm{B}_{D}=\mathbbm{B}_{D^{\prime}}
, i.e., the sets of edges are the same including their direction. Such causal models are one the “rotation” of the other, but can be considered locally equal, sine the direction of edges involved in the interventions are the same. Nonetheless, they share the same signalling structure without restrictions on which maps one inserts in the labs.

6
Application to holography

In this section, we apply the mapping to Holographic tensor networks, showing that properties of the graph underlying the image causal model straightforwardly imply the absence of causal influence in the tensor network.

6.1
The holographic tensor network

In AdS-CFT
[
Mal98
,
Wit98
]
, a duality is conjectured between a
(d+1)
-dimensional quantum gravity theory in Anti de-Sitter (AdS) space, called the
bulk theory
, and a
d
-dimensional conformal field theory (CFT) which lives on a space isomorphic to the conformal boundary of AdS, called the
boundary theory
. This correspondence implies a connection between degrees of freedom in the bulk and the boundary. Toy models for AdS-CFT construct quantum error correction codes where the degrees of freedom in the bulk are non-locally encoded in the boundary theory
[
ADH14
]
. These are known as
holographic tensor networks

[
PYHP15
,
HNQ+16
]
.

Perfect tensors and networks

The building block of holographic tensor networks are so-called
perfect tensors
.

Definition 19

(Perfect tensor
[
PYHP15
]
)
.

Consider a tensor of rank
2n
with indices
\mathcal{I}=\{1,\dots,2n\}
where the
j
-th index takes values
i_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}
, i.e.,

T=\{T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{2n}}\in\mathbb{C}\}_{i_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j},\;j=1,\dots,2n},

(99)

and define Hilbert spaces associated to each index
j\in\mathcal{I}
as
\mathcal{H}_{j}=\textup{span}\{|i_{j}\rangle_{j}\}_{i_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}}
.

The tensor
T
is a
perfect tensor
if any bipartition of indices into sets
S
and
\bar{S}
such that
|S|\leq|\bar{S}|
defines an isometry from
\mathcal{H}_{S}\equiv\bigotimes_{j\in S}\mathcal{H}_{j}
to
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{S}}\equiv\bigotimes_{k\in\bar{S}}\mathcal{H}_{k}
up to a constant. Explicitly, for all
S,\bar{S}\subseteq\mathcal{I}
, such that
S\cup\bar{S}=\mathcal{I}
,
S\cap\bar{S}=\emptyset
and
|S|\leq|\bar{S}|
, the linear operator

V=C\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{2n}}T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{2n}}\bigotimes_{j\in\bar{S}}|i_{j}\rangle_{j}\bigotimes_{k\in S}\langle i_{k}|_{k}\equiv C\sum_{i_{S},i_{\bar{S}}}T_{i_{S},i_{\bar{S}}}|i_{\bar{S}}\rangle_{\bar{S}}\langle i_{S}|_{S}

(100)

satisfies
V^{\dagger}V=\mathbbm{1}_{S}
, for some constant
C
. Notice that we have introduced a compact notation, where
i_{S}\equiv\{i_{j}\}_{j\in S}
and
|\psi\rangle_{S}\in\mathcal{H}_{S}
and similarly for
\bar{S}
.

For example, consider
n=3
,
\mathcal{I}=\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}
and the tensor
T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{6}}
, represented as a vector (see
Remark

1
)

{{}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -26.59 15.35}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\ \equiv|\Psi\rangle\langle\Psi|_{\mathcal{I}},\textup{ where }|\Psi\rangle_{\mathcal{I}}=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{6}}T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{6}}|i_{1}\rangle_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes|i_{6}\rangle_{6}.

(101)

Let us consider the bipartition
S=\{5,6\}
and
\bar{S}=\{1,2,3,4\}
, the perfect tensor property implies that the linear map

V=C\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{6}}T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{6}}|i_{1}\rangle_{1}\otimes|i_{2}\rangle_{2}\otimes|i_{3}\rangle_{3}\otimes|i_{4}\rangle_{4}\otimes\langle i_{5}|_{5}\otimes\langle i_{6}|_{6}

(102)

satisfies
V^{\dagger}V=\mathbbm{1}_{S}
for some constant
C
. Therefore, it is an isometry and defines an isometric channel
\mathcal{V}_{\bar{S}|S}(\rho_{S})=V\rho_{S}V^{\dagger}
.

Lemma 10

(Isometric channel of a perfect tensor)
.

Given a perfect tensor
T
of rank
2n
, to which we associate the state

|\Psi\rangle_{\mathcal{I}}=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{2n}}T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{2n}}|i_{1}\rangle_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes|i_{2n}\rangle_{2n},

(103)

and a bipartition of its indices into
S
and
\bar{S}
such that
|S|\leq|\bar{S}|
, the corresponding isometric channel, up to a constant
C
, is given by

C\mathbb{J}^{-1}_{\bar{S}|S}(|\Psi\rangle\langle\Psi|_{S\bar{S}})(\cdot)=V(\cdot)V^{\dagger}.

(104)

Proof.

See
Appendix

A
.
∎

Given the perfect tensor which we considered before with
n=3
, we can use
Definition

19
to define isometric channels for any bipartition with the correct cardinality, for instance:

\begin{split}\bar{S}=1,2,3,4,5,\;S=6;\;&V_{|S|=1}=C_{1}\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{6}}T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{6}}|i_{1}\rangle_{1}\otimes|i_{2}\rangle_{3}\otimes|i_{3}\rangle_{3}\otimes|i_{4}\rangle_{4}\otimes|i_{5}\rangle_{5}\otimes\langle i_{6}|_{6}\\
\bar{S}=1,2,3,4,\;S=5,6;\;&V_{|S|=2}=C_{2}\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{6}}T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{6}}|i_{1}\rangle_{1}\otimes|i_{2}\rangle_{3}\otimes|i_{3}\rangle_{3}\otimes|i_{4}\rangle_{4}\otimes\langle i_{5}|_{5}\otimes\langle i_{6}|_{6}\\
\bar{S}=1,2,3,\;S=4,5,6;\;&V_{|S|=3}=C_{3}\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{6}}T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{6}}|i_{1}\rangle_{1}\otimes|i_{2}\rangle_{3}\otimes|i_{3}\rangle_{3}\otimes\langle i_{4}|_{4}\otimes\langle i_{5}|_{5}\otimes\langle i_{6}|_{6}\end{split}

(105)

and so on.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\ \equiv|\Xi\rangle\langle\Xi|

(106)

is obtained through contracting perfect tensors with
2n=6
indices, like the one in
equation

101
. Notice that the above tensor network is not fully contracted, as the tensors on the boundary have open legs representing uncontracted indices. Therefore, the network is itself associated with a tensor with as many indices as the open edges. In the above example, we would have
H=\{H_{i_{1},\dots,i_{24},j_{1},\dots,j_{18}}\}
, where the indices
i_{l}
are associated to the open legs of the third layer of perfect tensors (four for each tensor) and the indices
j_{m}
to the open legs of the second layer of perfect tensors (three for each tensor).
Each perfect tensor is associated with a pure state
|\Psi\rangle\langle\Psi|
, thus the whole network itself is also associated with a pure state on a larger Hilbert space, which we denote as
|\Xi\rangle\langle\Xi|
. We often call the
bulk
of the network the contracted edges, and the
boundary
the uncontracted edges in
equation

106
.

In what follows, we are interested in studying operational causal influence of the network. For that purpose, one has to consider the fully contracted network that is obtained through contracting the network
H
with its complex conjugate.
In this case, one can prove that the network contraction equals

\langle L|\rho_{P}|L\rangle=|\langle\Xi|\Xi\rangle|^{2}.

(107)

In what follows, we only draw diagrams representing the uncontracted network, but study properties of the fully contracted network.

Causal influence in the Holographic tensor network

Studying causal influence in the Holographic tensor network corresponds to choosing two regions as subsets of contracted edges of the perfect network and then considering the relevant correlation functions. We denote the state corresponding to a choice of unitary and hermitian instrument,
U_{\mathcal{A}}
and
O_{\mathcal{B}}
, as:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\ .

(108)

The un-normalised state
|\Xi(U_{\mathcal{A}},O_{\mathcal{B}})\rangle
satisfies:

\langle L|(U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes O_{\mathcal{B}})\rho_{P}(U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes O^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{B}})|L\rangle=|\langle\Xi(U_{\mathcal{A}},O_{\mathcal{B}})|\Xi(U_{\mathcal{A}},O_{\mathcal{B}})\rangle|^{2}.

(109)

If we are able to express the unitary acting in the bulk (a subset of contracted edges) of the network as a, possibly different, unitary acting at the boundary (a subset of uncontracted edges), i.e.,

|\Xi(U_{\mathcal{A}},O_{\mathcal{B}})\rangle=\tilde{U}_{\text{boundary}}|\Xi(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}},O_{\mathcal{B}})\rangle\equiv\tilde{U}|\tilde{\Xi}(O_{\mathcal{B}})\rangle,

(110)

the causal influence from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
is trivially zero

Indeed, if this is the case

\begin{split}M(U_{\mathcal{A}}:O_{\mathcal{B}}|\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}})&=\langle L|(U_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes O_{\mathcal{B}})\rho_{P}(U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes O^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{B}})|L\rangle\\
&=|\langle\Xi(U_{\mathcal{A}},O_{\mathcal{B}})|\Xi(U_{\mathcal{A}},O_{\mathcal{B}})\rangle|^{2}\\
&=|\langle\tilde{\Xi}(O_{\mathcal{B}})|\tilde{U}^{\dagger}\tilde{U}|\tilde{\Xi}(O_{\mathcal{B}})\rangle|^{2}=|\langle\tilde{\Xi}(O_{\mathcal{B}})|\tilde{\Xi}(O_{\mathcal{B}})\rangle|^{2}\end{split}

(111)

for all unitaries
U_{\mathcal{A}}
and Hermitian instruments
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}}
.

In the context of tensor networks, this is achieved by “pushing” the unitary to the boundary.
Indeed, isometries
V
arising from perfect tensors have the property that any operator
O
acting on an appropriate subset of its edges can be replaced by an equal norm operator
O^{\prime}
acting on the remaining edges, because

VO=VOV^{\dagger}V=(VOV^{\dagger})V\equiv O^{\prime}V.

(112)

Thus, in the Holographic network, operators in the bulk can be pushed to the boundary. In
[
PYHP15
]
, it is shown how one can push the operator using only the circuit contained in a so-called
minimal geodesics
, which can be understood as a line from two points in the boundary bounding a region containing the operator we wish to push, and cutting the minimum amount of edges. For instance, in the tensor network on the left there is one bulk operator, denoted with the red circle. The corresponding geodesics is drawn with the dashed line. Then, according to
[
PYHP15
]
, the operator can be pushed to the boundary as in the right network:

(113)

However, in some instances the procedure to “push” the networks cannot be performed, thus leaving the question on whether there is causal influence unsolved. Consider the following example (reproduced from Figure 18 of
[
CHQY19
]
) where we denote with a red circle the region
\mathcal{A}
and with a blue square the region
\mathcal{B}
:

|\Xi(U_{\mathcal{A}},O_{\mathcal{B}})\rangle\langle\Xi(U_{\mathcal{A}},O_{\mathcal{B}})|=\ \vbox{\hbox{\hbox to140.37pt{\vbox to132.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 70.18196pt\lower-66.01634pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\ .

(114)

Here, one cannot push the unitary operator, represented as a circle, through the boundary. Indeed, the presence of the tensors in
\mathcal{B}
, represented as blue squares, inside the geodesics prevents us from using the perfect tensor property to push the
\mathcal{A}
operator to the boundary. Thus, from the geodesic criterion alone, one cannot conclude whether
\mathcal{A}
influences
\mathcal{B}
in this case.

The goal of the next sections will be to map the tensor network to a causal model, analyse signalling and use it to infer causal influence in the tensor network.
Since each hexagon is a perfect tensor, we can freely choose the input-output systems to apply
Definition

16
and obtain a causal model, the only requirement being that our choice is consistent for the whole network and that the number of output systems is smaller than the number of input systems. However, the latter requirement, which exploits the perfect tensor property, is not necessary. In general, one can apply the generalised mapping of
Definition

16
and still obtain a valid cyclic causal model when the input and output choice does not define an isometric channel but generally a CP.

This freedom allows us to make choices that simplify studying signalling, for instance with graph-separation theorems, and to consider models which do not respect the perfect tensor property, at the expense of possibly adding cycles.

6.2
Mapping to causal models and application of graph-separation theorems

In the previous sections, we constructed a mapping from tensor networks to causal models which preserves causal influence as time-reversible signalling in the causal model. Here, we use the mapping to apply graph-separation results, proven for causal models, to argue for the absence of causal influence from one region to another. Let us first provide an intuition behind graph separation results (for more details we refer to
Appendix

D
).

Graph-separation theorems

Graph separation properties, such as
d
-separation
[
Pea09
]
, are purely defined at the level of a graph, irrespective of causal models living on it. The
d
-separation property, for instance, says that two sets of vertices
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are either
d
-separated or
d
-connected conditioned on a third set of vertices,
V_{3}
, and we denote it respectively as
(V_{1}\perp^{d}V_{2}|V_{3})
or
(V_{1}\not\perp^{d}V_{2}|V_{3})
.

For example, in the graph,
A\rightarrow C\leftarrow B
, called a
collider graph
,
A
and
B
are
d
-separated but they become
d
-connected conditioned on
C
, hence
A\perp^{d}B
and
A\not\perp^{d}B|C
.
In acyclic graphs, the
d
-separation theorem states that:
(Soundness)
if
(V_{1}\perp^{d}V_{2}|V_{3})
, then the probability distribution over the outcomes of these vertices also presents the same conditional independence in
any
causal model on the graph, and
(Completeness)
if
(V_{1}\not\perp^{d}V_{2}|V_{3})
, then
there exists
a causal model on the graph whose distribution exhibits the corresponding conditional dependence.

For cyclic graphs, this theorem typically fails
[
Pea09
,
Nea00
]
, but a different graph-separation property,
p
-separation, has been proven to be sound and complete for all finite-dimensional cyclic causal models
[
FGV25b
]
.
Thus, both for cyclic and acyclic causal models one can apply graph separation theorems to conclude conditional independencies, which are fundamental to determine no-signalling relation, solely from the structure of the graph.

Causal model of a Holographic tensor network

We now use our mapping from tensor networks to causal models to immediately conclude that there is no causal influence from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
in the following example, where we label the tensors for clarity:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\ ,

(116)

and use the mapping from tensor networks to causal models to define a model on the above graph. Notice that the graph above is acyclic and the mapping leads to a valid causal model for each tensor as the number of chosen inputs of each tensor are less or equals than those of the outputs.

The mapping also provides us with causal mechanisms associated to each vertex of the graph. On the other hand, we can apply graph-separation theorems to the obtained graph to already infer no-signalling properties. Indeed, as can be easily proven using the definition of
d
-separation (see
Appendix

D
), the vertex represented by the red circle is
d
-separated from the blue squares. More intuitively, this
d
-separation captures that, relative to the partial order induced by the acyclic causal model, none of the blue squares are in the future of the red circle (and vice versa), and we would thus expect that the red circle cannot signal to the blue squares. This is indeed ensured by the
d
-separation theorem. Because of the soundness of
d
-separation, any causal model on the graph will not show correlations between the random variable associated to the labs
\mathcal{A}
and the one associated to the labs
\mathcal{B}
, i.e.,

\mathrm{Pr}{(y|a,b)_{\textup{I}}}=\mathrm{Pr}{(y|a^{\prime},b)_{\textup{I}}}

(117)

for all
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{A}
.
7
7

7

More precisely, while
d
-separation of the red circle from the blue squares used above is a sufficient condition to ensure no-signalling from
\mathcal{A}
(red circle) to
\mathcal{B}
(blue squares), a tighter condition would be to include a parentless node, say
a
pointing to the red circle (modelling the free choice of operation performed at
\mathcal{A}
) and consider whether
a
is
d
-separated from the blue squares. Indeed, we can have situations where the blue squares are
d
-connected to the red circle (e.g., when the red circle lies in the “future” of the blue squares or equivalently when there exists a directed path from the blue squares to the red circle) but remain
d
-separated from the parentless node
a
. Even in this case, the
d
-separation theorem would guarantee no-signalling as it would prevent
a
from getting correlated with the outcomes
y
at the blue squares.
Using
Theorem

8
, proving that there is causal influence from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
if and only if there is signalling, one can immediately conclude that there is no causal influence from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
.

Observe that the method used above consists in choosing a mapping from tensor networks to causal models and construct the corresponding graph including the relevant in intervention. In this example, we were able to construct an acyclic graph and use the
d
-separation theorem to infer no signalling directly from the connectivity of the graph. If the graph is cyclic, which is the case for a general mapping, one can use
p
-separation
[
FGV25b
]
instead. For instance, consider the example of Figure 18 in
[
CHQY19
]
:

|\Xi(U_{\mathcal{A}},O_{\mathcal{B}})\rangle\langle\Xi(U_{\mathcal{A}},O_{\mathcal{B}})|=\ \vbox{\hbox{\hbox to140.37pt{\vbox to132.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 70.18196pt\lower-66.01634pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 70.87 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 70.87 C 14.02 62.77 27.32 63.51 41.34 71.6}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(118)

One can easily see that there is no choice of directions between the input-output spaces of
a
and
b.5
that would allow to make use of the perfect tensor property and have all blue squares outside the future of the red circle, with respect to the partial order induced by the causal model. Indeed, depending on the choice of direction of the edge
\{a,b.5\}
either the tensor
a
or the tensor
b.5
would require to have more inputs than outputs in order to ensure that the blue squares are in the past of the unitary.

We can make use of the generalised mapping to map the network to a cyclic causal model. Let us focus on the tensors
a
and
b.5
only, as one can easily see that a consistent choice of directions for the other tensors, which also fulfils the input-output dimension requirements, can always be found.
8
8

8

This is the case because the two tensors in consideration have at most one edge in common with any other tensor. Hence, for both directions of such edge one can choose the remaining directions on the other tensor to have valid dimensions and to be consistent with the rest of the network. See, for instance, the previous example.

Consider the two choices of directions for the edge
\{a,b.5\}
:

(119)

This represents a specific choice of directions, in general the direction of the
\{a,b.5\}
and the requirement that all blue square operators should not be in the causal past of the unitary imposes requirements on the edges involving operators. Specifically all edges of this kind that are connected to the tensor where
\{a,b.5\}
is ingoing, also have to be ingoing. In the left choice of the diagram, this constraint is applied in
b.5
and in the diagram on the right it is applied in
a
. The other directions can be freely chosen in each case respectively, and the diagrams above are just an example of choice.

In both options one of the tensors certainly does not fulfil the perfect tensor properties, as it has four inputs and two outputs. Thus, when mapping it to a causal model we have to make use of the generalised mapping of
Definition

16
, which introduces a self cycle in the vertex of such tensor, leading to:

Signalling relations in these graph can be addressed using a different graph separation property,
p
-separation, which is sound and complete for finite dimensional cyclic causal models
9
9

9

Tensor networks are also defined for finite dimensional, discrete indices, hence this is not restrictive. The settings labelling the interventions that we consider might be infinite dimensional. However, one can easily see that systems, i.e., Hilbert spaces or indices, associated to edges which are not involved in loops, may not be finite without compromising the results of
[
FGV25b
]
. Settings are associated to exogenous vertices, thus there are never involved in loops.
. In this example, one can prove that the self-loops
p
-connect the unitary to the region denoted with squares.
By the
p
-separation theorem
[
FGV25b
]
, this means that generically, there can be signalling in causal models on such a graph (and by our results, causal influence in the associated tensor network), except in the case of certain fine-tuned causal models. Checking whether or not the scenario involves such fine-tuning that washes out the signalling (or operational causal influence), would require explicit calculation of the associated quantities by applying
Definition

12
(or
Definition

17
) for the given causal model (or tensor network), and cannot be solely determined by analysing the graph.

Remark 8
.

While the holographic models for tensor networks heavily rely on the perfect tensor property for analysing causal influence, causal model methods can be applied to arbitrary tensors. In case the mapping of
Definition

16
can be applied, one might be able to construct an acyclic graph and make considerations using the
d
-separation theorem. More generally, one can apply
Definition

16
and obtain a cyclic causal model with self-loops added to a subset of vertices, as prescribed by the generalised mapping, and use the
p
-separation theorem, which generalises
d
-separation to cyclic scenarios.

7
Discussion and outlook

In this work, we established explicit mappings between tensor networks and quantum causal models, enabling a causal interpretation of arbitrary tensor networks (as detailed in the summary of contributions in
Section

1.1
). In particular, this makes it possible to apply causal reasoning backed by sound and complete graph-separation theorems even in the absence of any a priori notion of directionality.

We showed that a causal model uniquely determines a tensor network, while the inverse mapping is non-unique, with a single tensor network corresponding to multiple distinct causal models on fundamentally different causal graphs (depending on a choice of orientation for each undirected edge). These mappings motivated the introduction of an operational notion of causal influence for tensor networks. A notable consequence is the existence of families of genuinely different models sharing identical no-signalling relations, suggesting a form of causal equivalence. Finally, we illustrated the use of this framework by applying causal-inference tools to holographic tensor networks to infer causal influence.

We now discuss interesting future directions for building on this framework and results:

•

Rotations of causal models:
The discovery of equivalence classes of “rotated” causal models, with the same signalling structure under time-symmetric interventions but different causal graphs, opens several interesting questions: which properties of causal models are preserved under such rotations? are there novel properties arising through this equivalence, similarly to how dual unitaries exhibit interesting features along light-like directions
[
PBCP20
,
ZH22
]
?
In addition, this result suggests a potential connection between fine-tuned relations in one causal model and sound graph-separation properties in another representative of the same equivalence class.
Indeed, the observed non-signalling relations may arise from different sources across the equivalence class, for instance from
d
- or
p
-separation
[
Pea09
,
FGV25b
]
or from fine-tuning, depending on the underlying causal structure.

•

Graph-separation theorem for tensor networks on undirected graphs:
Graph-separation criteria, such as
d
- and
p
-separation, are defined for directed graphs and proven to be sound and complete for subsets causal models. One might wonder whether these criteria induce a corresponding separation theorem for tensor networks defined on undirected graphs via the mappings developed in this work. Preliminary evidence contained in the tensor-network examples of
Section

6.2
suggests that this may indeed be the case. For instance, in the tensor network of
equation

118
, the squared region
\mathcal{B}
cannot be separated from the unitary
\mathcal{A}
under any orientation of edges that yields a valid causal model through our original mapping: the fact that
\mathcal{B}
occupies three edges of the same tensor to which
\mathcal{A}
is connected prevents a choice of input–output directions consistent with the perfect-tensor property that would
d
-separate the two regions. While a generalised mapping allowing additional cyclicity can be constructed, the resulting self-cycles
p
-connect
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, again preventing separation. By contrast, in the tensor network of
equation

115
, where two regions share fewer edges with a common tensor, suitable edge orientations exist that yield an acyclic causal model in which the regions are
d
-separated and hence causally disconnected. This suggests that one could define a purely graph-theoretic separation criterion for undirected graphs that might be sound and complete for tensor networks, inheriting graph-separation properties of the associated equivalence class of causal models.
In the special case of holographic tensor networks, one could formally relate such property to the geodesic-based criterion mentioned above
[
PYHP15
,
CHQY19
]
.

•

Emergence of space-time from operational properties:
Tensor networks are used to define the geometry of space-time in terms of the structure of the network, which determines properties such as its curvature and the distance between two points. For instance, the so-called “MERA” tensor network
[
Vid08
]
, with its specific local and global structure, has been studied to define distance in terms of the number of edges that must be traversed to connect two tensors of the network, thus inferring how a fundamental concept like distance might arise from the connectivity of processes. More generally, defining space-time properties using tensor networks remains an active area of research
[
May17
,
CHQY19
,
Vid08
]
, which provides new insights into the fundamental nature of space-time and its relationship to other physical phenomena. Our framework maps a given tensor network to a causal model, a connection that could allow us to understand space-time properties emerging in tensor networks as well-known operational properties in causal models, and to be able to consistently apply powerful graph-theoretic methods from causal models to such problems.

•

Indefinite causal order:

Our work allows one to connect so-called indefinite causal order (ICO) processes or higher-order quantum operations
[
OCB12
,
CDPV13
]
to tensor network models, since these are known to be a subset of cyclic quantum causal structures
[
AGB17
]
that can be causally modelled in the framework of
[
FGV25b
]
used here. This would lend further techniques and ideas for exploring realisations of such processes in regimes beyond classical space-time considered in
[
VR24a
,
VR24b
]
, including quantum gravitational regimes where space-time geometry can exhibit quantum superposition
[
ZCPB19
,
CRGBB20
,
PV20
,
SMSY24
,
VCYR25
,
KHA+25
]
.
Further developing the links between tensor network and causal models established here, could contribute to connecting tensor-network models of quantum gravity with quantum causal structures and higher-order quantum operations, offering a more unified language to describe causality beyond fixed causal orderings. Finally, it would also be interesting to consider the application of these ideas to study the conditions under which definite causal and temporal order may emerge from a world that allows arbitrary indefinite causal order processes.
